# Fork notes

This is a private-use fork of [jazzenchen/agent-hud-open](https://github.com/jazzenchen/agent-hud-open). All of the fork's changes live on the branch `fork`, which is the default branch here. `main` mirrors upstream and never carries fork commits. Pull requests target `fork`. Nothing is sent upstream.

## Differences from upstream

Add one row for every difference the fork introduces.

| Area | Change | Files | Reason | Ticket |
| ---- | ------ | ----- | ------ | ------ |
| Sound | Core decides which moments deserve a sound: `needs-you` when a session starts waiting on the user (a request, a question, or a wait its hooks report), `finished` when a turn ends; steps make none, and each alert has an id played once. Several requests that newly wait in one session at one refresh make one `needs-you` (one ping per session per refresh); none of them sounds later on its own. Which requests a session waits on is read before cards hide transcript sightings, so the panel shows exactly what upstream shows: a card and a sighting are one request when both name the same tool call (a hook's `tool_use_id`), or, where the card names none (Claude Code's `PermissionRequest`), while both are there at the same refresh as the only possible pairing; otherwise they are two, preferring an occasional extra sound to a silenced question. A request is new only if none of its names was decided before; all are checked before any is marked, so a new card first seen beside its own call's sighting still sounds once. Only a top-level session's turn end is `finished`: a subagent's makes none, known by its `parentId` or, where its log names no parent (a Codex review or compaction), by the new optional `liveFacts.subagent: true`, which only such sessions carry. No playback yet. | `src/crates/core/src/sound_alerts.rs`, `core/src/sound_alerts/requests.rs`, `core/src/sound_alerts/tests.rs`, `core/src/sound_alerts/tests/named_card.rs`, `core/src/presentation.rs`, `core/src/presentation/requests.rs`, `core/src/snapshot.rs`, `core/src/snapshot/session_rows.rs`, `core/src/requests/views.rs` (`calls`), `core/src/settings.rs`, `core/src/lib.rs`, `core/src/demo/tests.rs`, `agents/src/hooks/request.rs` (`call_id`), `agents/src/hooks/request/tests.rs`, `agents/src/transcript/session_state.rs` (`subagent`), `core/src/presentation/tests.rs`, `src/packages/protocol/src/{settings,requests,snapshot,session}.ts`, `docs/parity/c09-app-settings.md` row 40b, `docs/parity/README.md` totals | Upstream removed its sound on 2026-10-21 (parity c09 #40b); Nicky wants to hear when an agent needs him or finishes without watching the panel. | 05, 47 |
| Sound playback | The host plays each `soundAlerts` id once, by id not revision, as a macOS system sound (`/System/Library/Sounds/<name>.aiff` through `afplay`, on a thread of its own), only while `sound` is on. Each play or failure is one line in `sound.log` in the app's data folder (kind, sound, alert id). `native_play_sound` plays a named sound now for Settings' preview. Windows and Linux report `sound.supported: false` and play nothing until playback is verified there. The demo plays too, but only after sound is turned on in its own settings, which start off on every launch; its log is in `demo-run/`. | `src/crates/host/src/system/{sound,mod,macos,windows,linux}.rs`, `docs/platform-architecture.md`, `host/src/backend.rs`, `host/src/lib.rs`, `src/packages/protocol/src/snapshot.ts`, `src/crates/host/src/system/sound_tests.rs` | Upstream removed its sound on 2026-10-21 (parity c09 #40b); Nicky wants to hear when an agent needs him or finishes. No audio files are added: the system's own sounds are used. | 06 |
| Sound settings | General settings has a Sound section: a "Play sounds" switch (`sound`) and a system sound for "Needs you" (`soundNeedsYou`) and for "Finished" (`soundFinished`). Choosing a sound saves it, then plays it once through `native_play_sound`. The pickers are disabled while the switch is off; where the host plays no sound, the switch is disabled and says so in the app's language ("Sound isn't played on this system yet.", `sound-reason.ts`, since ticket 66; before, it showed the host's notice, an English literal). The choices are the names the host reports as `connection.system.sound.names` (macOS: `/System/Library/Sounds/*.aiff`, sorted), so the list is never kept in TypeScript. | `src/packages/app/src/settings/{SoundSection,GeneralPane}.tsx`, `src/packages/app/src/settings/sound-reason.ts`, `src/packages/app/test/sound-reason.test.ts`, `src/crates/host/src/system/{macos,mod}.rs`, `src/packages/protocol/src/snapshot.ts`, `docs/parity/c09-app-settings.md` row 40b, `docs/parity/README.md` totals | Upstream has no sound setting since 2026-10-21 (parity c09 #40b); Nicky wants to mute sound in class and pick sounds that don't annoy him. | 07 |

| Launch at login | On macOS, with the setting on, launch registers the fork when `SMAppService` reports 0 (not registered) or 3 (not found), and keeps macOS's answer and reason. Settings draws the switch from the OS status (`login.enabled`), not the saved setting. Status 3 with no failed registration shows plain off. When the registration fails, or the item is still not found after it, the switch shows off with a warning (macOS's reason, or upstream's "This build can't find the login service."), although the setting stays saved as on. Turning it off from 0 or 3 does nothing. Parity row c04 #91 says so. **Since upstream 0.6.7 (ticket 66):** upstream's language rewrite made Settings show a stock line for both warnings, which hid macOS's reason and the name of the app to approve. The host now words both from the catalogs, in the app's language at each reading (`Failure::words`, `report`), with macOS's reason and the app's name filled in, and Settings shows the host's words. The host keeps the status it read, words and all, so it keeps the language those words were made in beside it and reads the status again for a snapshot in another language: a change of the app's language changes the warning too (`system/mod.rs`, `a_change_of_language_words_the_kept_warning_again`) (`GeneralPane.tsx`; `the_warnings_are_in_the_apps_language_with_macos_reason_and_the_apps_name`, `launch-at-login-render.test.ts` with the real pane) | `src/crates/host/src/system/macos.rs`, `docs/parity/c04-models-logic.md`, `src/packages/app/src/settings/GeneralPane.tsx`, `src/packages/app/test/launch-at-login-render.test.ts` | The main app reports 3 until it is first registered, and registering from 3 works: on the installed fork, turning the switch on from 3 registered `com.nickymolina.agenthud` at `/Applications/Agent HUD Fork.app`, enabled. Upstream's code is the same, so its open build likely has the gap too; not checked, as the official app is never run. macOS files the login item under the code signature's identifier, which for `fork-install.sh`'s linker-signed build is `agent_hud-325ef49821968768`, not the bundle id: a build whose identifier changes gets a new record, and the old one keeps its state | 43 |

| Hermes usage | A running session's usage rows count as running totals: the first counts stay at `first_seen`, and later growth is its own event per 15-minute period, dated at the read, or at the row's `last_seen` when that moved past the previous read's stamp (Open always dates it at the read). | Hermes | Declares Hermes's local gateway as a native channel that carries requests, and adds its pure half: the shapes, frames to cards, decisions to frames, and the polls that keep the cards matching what Hermes says waits. The connection is the next row. | `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/mod.rs`, `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/`, `src/crates/agents/tests/fixtures/hermes/gateway/`, `docs/agent-contract.md`, `docs/third-party.md` | Hermes's shell hooks only observe. | Hermes | The connection to Hermes's local gateway: finds the control socket in the Hermes home, gets a ticket as the same user, opens the WebSocket, runs the model's polls and does what it says. A question or an approval Hermes waits on shows as a request card within a second and goes when Hermes settles it; an answer given on the card is sent on a socket of its own. It starts and stops with the client hooks setting, reconnects when the gateway restarts, leaves a gateway without the live connection to the hooks, and logs to `hermes-gateway.log` in the data folder. It never writes under a Hermes home and never keeps a ticket. | `src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/`, `src/crates/platform/src/clients/mod.rs`, `src/crates/platform/src/hooks/mod.rs`, `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/connection.rs` (an origin that is not plain http on this machine is unsupported) | The model of ticket 12 connected nothing. See "The connection, as built" below. | 13 |
| Hermes approvals | A permission card draws one button for each lasting choice whose suggestion Core labels (`{type: "scope", scope, label}`) and sends that suggestion back whole as `{kind: "always", suggestion}`; with none labelled, a card keeps its one Always allow button, as upstream. A Hermes approval shows Deny, Allow this session, Always allow and Allow once, each only where Hermes offers it, so a limited request shows Deny and Allow once. The words come from Core, in both languages. | `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/card.rs`, `src/packages/hud/src/permissions/{PermissionToolCard.tsx,permission-choices.ts}`, `src/packages/hud/test/permission-choices.test.ts`, `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/tests/buttons.rs`, `src/crates/agents/tests/fixtures/hermes/approval-card-buttons.json`, `src/crates/core/src/requests/tests.rs`, `src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/tests/approvals.rs` | Hermes has four answers to an approval, where upstream's card has three buttons. | Hermes state | While Hermes's gateway is followed, a session it lists shows running, waiting or idle as the gateway lists it, in place of the hooks' guess: a long tool call stays running past two minutes, and a question stays waiting until it is answered. A session with a card waits even where Hermes lists it working. Sessions the gateway does not list, and every session while the gateway is not ready, a profile's list just failed, or no poll has read it since it was last ready, keep today's hook and log states. So does every session once the newest successful reading is 30 s old (`LIVE_STATES_MS`), even if the channel says nothing more, and a session whose hooks reported a turn's start or end after the reading, until the next reading. Core holds the states and when they were read as a generic native-channel fact (`Requests::set_native_states`, `agents::LiveStates`) beside the native waiting flags; a card still wins over them. | `src/crates/agents/src/channel.rs`, `agents/src/lib.rs`, `agents/src/hermes/gateway/{live,effect,state,reconcile,mod,tests}.rs`, `agents/src/hermes/gateway/tests/live.rs`, `core/src/requests.rs`, `core/src/requests/views.rs`, `core/src/presentation.rs`, `core/src/presentation/live.rs`, `core/src/hook_events.rs` (`reported_at`), `core/src/source_db.rs`, `core/src/source_db/hermes_live_tests.rs`, `docs/client-hooks.md` | Hermes's hooks report a turn's start and end and nothing between, so upstream lets a quiet turn lapse to idle after two minutes and a waiting one after the same. The gateway's list says what each session does at every poll. | 16 |
| Native readers | Core keeps what native channels tell it by source and reader, where upstream keeps one waiting list for a source and ticket 16 kept one reading. `set_native_states` and `set_native_waiting` take a reader name beside the source, and the new `end_native_reader` clears one reader's states and flags. Each reading has its own `read_ms`: a session follows the reading that lists it (the newer, if two list it; two of one moment are settled by the readers' names), its hooks are compared with that reading's time, and each reading lapses 30 s after its own time through core's own deadline. A reader's waiting list replaces only its own; a session waits while any reader lists it. Every channel there is today passes `agents::SOLE_READER` and behaves as before: upstream's Antigravity, Codex and DeepSeek call sites differ by that argument and its import, nothing else. | `src/crates/agents/src/channel.rs` (`SOLE_READER`), `agents/src/lib.rs`, `core/src/requests.rs`, `core/src/requests/native.rs` (new; also holds what ticket 16 had put in `views.rs`), `core/src/requests/views.rs`, `core/src/presentation/live.rs`, `core/src/source_db.rs`, `core/src/source_db/{hermes_live_tests,hermes_readers_tests}.rs`, `core/src/tests/hermes_questions.rs`, `platform/src/clients/{antigravity/permissions.rs,codex/desktop.rs,deepseek/questions.rs,deepseek/questions/observer.rs,hermes/effects.rs,hermes/tests/states.rs}`, `platform/src/deepseek_plugin/asks.rs`, `docs/client-hooks.md` | Phase 2 has a follower for each Hermes gateway. With one reading for the source, a far poll's newer time made this Mac's older "running" beat a completion hook that came after it, and with the older time one stalled follower lapsed a healthy one (design review of ticket 20, finding 1). Core has no client's name in it: a reader is any name a channel gives. | 24 |
| Hermes questions | A Hermes question's card is the existing question card. Its header names the profile as its sessions do ("Hermes Agent · <profile>"), and so does an approval's card (ticket 15's `tests/fixtures/hermes/approval-card-buttons.json` records that label). A question of a set that another viewer already answered or skipped says so under its text (`lock` on `ApprovalQuestion`, a generic optional field), can be passed without an answer to keep it, has no Skip, and is struck through once answered here. A lone Hermes question has no Skip (`canSkip: false` on `Approval`, generic; Core then refuses a decision that leaves a question unanswered): no answer a viewer can send skips it everywhere, as Hermes's own skip value is internal and the empty answer its contract calls a skip is, where the gateway runs the turn itself, a timeout that also drops the questions still to come in the call. Its card keeps the options, the typed reply and "Return to session". Skip in a set settles the set: each question skipped here goes as `null`, a locked one left alone keeps its answer, and the frame always carries `answers`. An open request card shows the whole name in its header, where upstream cuts every name at 150 px: an open question's name takes the row, and an open card that also shows a summary (an approval) keeps its name whole up to 58% of the row, the summary giving way first (ticket 42). Closed queue rows keep the cut. The `needs-you` sound comes from the existing alert model, with no Hermes code. | `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/card.rs`, `gateway/decision.rs`, `gateway/tests/`, `src/crates/core/src/requests/answer.rs`, `src/crates/core/src/tests/hermes_questions.rs`, `src/packages/protocol/src/requests.ts`, `src/packages/hud/src/permissions/PermissionQuestionCard.tsx`, `permissions/QuestionLockNote.tsx`, `permissions/question-answers.ts`, `permissions/QuestionOptions.tsx`, `src/packages/hud/src/alerts/PendingEventRows.tsx`, `alerts/row-head-label.ts`, `src/packages/hud/test/question-answers.test.ts`, `test/row-head-label.test.ts`, `docs/parity/c07-glow-alerts-theme.md` (#85, `better`), `docs/parity/README.md` | Answer Hermes's questions in the HUD (spec stories 10 to 13). A Skip that Core accepts takes the card away before Hermes hears of it, so every Skip the card offers must reach Hermes as a real skip. The profile was cut off in the header ("Hermes Agent · hud-spi…"), on question cards and then on approval cards. | 14, 42 |
| Native card rows | A waiting card reaches its session's row at the pass the card's wake asks for, not at the log's next read, for a client whose native channel says its transcript never shows a request it waits on (`Channel::transcript_shows_pending: false`, set only on Hermes's gateway, in `agents/src/hermes/mod.rs`). For such a client a row only hooks made takes its cards as a log row does (`Presentation::cards`, split out of the log rows' overlay), and a native card whose session has no row at all gets a waiting row (`evidence: "native-card"`, no tokens, the session's default title); a session the list leaves out on purpose (`hidden_from_list`) gets none. Once the log names the session its own row takes over under the same id; since that transcript shows no sighting of the card, nothing sounds again. Every other client's cards (Codex Desktop, Antigravity, DeepSeek) wait for its log exactly as upstream, and `sound_alerts` is upstream's. Measured in the lab: a Hermes question in a session started a moment before began its sound 1.9 to 3.9 s after its card before (`sound.log` 3.1 to 5.3 s), 0.05 to 0.44 s after. | `src/crates/agents/src/channel.rs` (`transcript_shows_pending`), `agents/src/{hermes,codex,deepseek,antigravity}/mod.rs`, `src/crates/core/src/presentation.rs` (`cards`), `core/src/snapshot/card_only_rows.rs`, `core/src/snapshot/session_rows.rs`, `core/src/snapshot.rs`, `core/src/requests/views.rs` (`native_sessions`), `core/src/clients/mod.rs` (`hidden_from_list`), `core/src/tests/native_card_rows.rs`, `core/src/tests/native_card_codex_as_fork.rs`, `core/src/tests/hermes_questions.rs`, `core/src/tests/mod.rs` | Upstream lists a session's wait only on its log's row, and the fork's `needs-you` follows the wait. Nicky should hear "needs you" as soon as a Hermes card is there. One entry's route owns it: the first entry in the list's order whose route verified as it. Every other entry that verifies as it, or as this Mac (the key of this Mac's own `install_id` file and root home), is "same machine" and follows nothing. A route change (an entry's address fields edited, or the owner removed while another entry reaches the machine) raises the machine's route number first, marks its cards in the same step, has a route verify anew, and then either goes on under that owner with the same requests, or, where every entry that could lead there verified as something else or was refused, retires the machine (cards withdrawn, readers ended) before the new machine is made. A route that cannot be reached is not another machine. The bindings (`hermes-machines.json` in the data folder: entry ids, address fields, keys, labels) say which machine an entry verified as before; nothing is dialled from them. The machines list (ticket 31) has one status for each entry, with the Hermes adapter's notes in both languages. An unknown or changed host key is tried again every five minutes, not every half minute. | `src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/machines.rs` and `machines/{book,keeper,change,apply,list,bindings,status,words}.rs` (new), `route/switch.rs` (new), `handover.rs` (new), `machine.rs`, `machine/{follow,alone}.rs`, `followers.rs`, `follower.rs`, `effects.rs`, `route/ssh/{keeper,notice}.rs`, `route/local.rs` (`key`), `mod.rs`, `src/crates/platform/src/hooks/mod.rs` (starts `Machines` in place of `Observer`), `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/machine.rs` (`INSTALL_ID_FILE`, `stored_install`), `src/crates/core/src/requests.rs` and `requests/probes.rs` (a test probe, off in the app), tests `clients/hermes/tests/machines.rs` and `tests/machines/`, `docs/parity/c03-hooks-store-ledger.md` #99, `docs/parity/README.md` | Phase 2 covers the gateways of every `ssh` entry in the desktop app's list (spec "Phase 2 design", sections 1 and 3), and the design ruled that a list that cannot be read changes nothing and that a machine has one owner with a handover. | 32 |

Each lab folder comes over the pipe with its device and inode; the watchdog holds it open (never through a link), empties it through that descriptor without following links, and removes its path only where the path is still that folder. | Hermes far rows, grown mirrors and failed reads | **Changes how this Mac's own Hermes sessions are read too.** (1) **A new session is read first.** A page of a Hermes `state.db` read a hundred sessions from the lowest rowid up and began again at every change, so a session that began was read last: one pass, two seconds apart, for every hundred sessions before it. A page now reads the sessions above the highest rowid read so far first, newest first, a hundred at most, and then goes on with the pass over the old ones; where more began than a page reads, later pages take the rest, newest first, each once. No session is read twice and none is left out, and a cursor saved by the reader before this goes on as it is. (2) **A mirror's pass over its old rows ends in the page that began it.** In a mirror, and only there, a page looks at up to 10,000 old rows where it looked at 100, and still reads 100 at most. Every write to a mirror begins that pass anew, and it cost one pass for each hundred rows, each at what a pass costs with that many sessions listed; now it costs the one pass that reads the new row. (3) The check whether a session's counts changed looks its rowid up before it puts it in: put in again, it cost as much as the map of counts is long. (4) **A file its writer says it wrote is read though its last read failed.** Core leaves a log it could not read be for 30 s, doubling to 300 s, and a file event does not end that. A native channel's word that it wrote the file now does: `AgentHudCore::written_files` takes the paths as `file_changes` does and ends their pause, and the runtime hands told files there. (5) **No mirror without its tables.** A mirror that is not there yet is made under another name (`state.db.new`), with its first transaction in it, closed, and only then given its own; a first write that is given up leaves no file. Before, the file was there from the moment before its first commit, and stayed, without tables, when that write was dropped at the route check or refused, for core to fail on at every retry. (6) **An `ssh` entry with no label goes by its host.** Its machine was followed and had cards, and no mirror could be claimed for it, so its sessions had no rows. (7) A mirror write that fails the same way at every poll is logged once, not at every poll. (8) A full write queue drops its oldest listing and never a label write, and a label change that waits already is not queued twice. "Grown mirrors, and reads that failed" below has the choice, the measurements and what tickets 35 and 37 must know. | `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/transcript.rs`, `transcript/rows.rs`, `transcript/usage.rs` (the reader's rows, moved out of `transcript.rs`), `session_counts.rs`, `connections.rs`; `src/crates/core/src/ingestion.rs`, `ingestion/waiting.rs`; `src/crates/runtime/src/request.rs`; `src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/mirror.rs`, `mirror/files.rs`, `mirror/work.rs`, `src/crates/platform/src/hooks/changed.rs`; `docs/agent-contract.md`, `docs/parity/` | Ticket 34's verifier found that a far session's row took one pass for every hundred rows already in its mirror (11 passes at 1,000 rows, about 20 s), that live rows are never removed so every mirror gets there, and that a mirror read at a moment it had no tables was not read again for 30 s. | Far history reader, how it ends | **The reader now ends every run with `os._exit`, and never through Python's own shutdown.** It crashed five times in the lab's loaded nights (segmentation fault at address 0x10, macOS system Python 3.9.6), and each crash is a failed read and a crash report on the far machine. Cause, read from the reports' stack, CPython 3.9.6's source and Apple's build of it: the reader's watchdog thread wakes every 0.5 ms and takes the interpreter lock. It first checks that Python is not shutting down, and on the next instruction reads its own thread state. If the system takes the thread off the processor between the two, and the main thread starts the shutdown meanwhile, that state is freed, and macOS zeroes freed memory: the thread reads a null pointer plus 0x10. The window is about a nanosecond, so it needs a thread parked there by a loaded machine. **Not reproduced:** 15,600 runs of the old reader under load (three loops) and about 10,000 runs of stand-ins made to widen other windows ended with no crash; the cause is an inference from those three sources, not a crash seen here. The fix does not depend on it: with no shutdown, nothing frees the thread's state while the process lives. Nothing is lost by it: every byte the reader says goes straight to the descriptor (`os.write`), the database is closed before the first byte, and status 0 still means it said what it had to. An interrupt in the main thread is now a crash line and status 1, where it was a traceback. Test: `deadline::the_reader_never_ends_through_pythons_own_shutdown` gives the far Python an exit hook that only the shutdown runs, and fails on the old ending. | `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/far_history/reader.py`, `src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/far_history/unix/tests/deadline.rs` | Each crash fails a read and leaves a crash report on the far machine, and the Mac Mini runs the same program | 55 |
| Hermes far alerts | **A far machine's sessions sound like this Mac's, within stated limits.** `needs-you` sounds once for every far question or approval, from its card, as for any session. `finished` sounds once for a far turn that ends while the HUD is connected to that machine and had seen the session before. This Mac's hooks say when a turn starts and ends; a far machine runs none, a session the list calls idle is no turn that ended, and the polls read only sessions that work, wait or show a card. So a far gateway's polls also take one light look at every other listed session (`session.events.since` with no cursor: where the stream is, and no events), the model keeps the place it last saw each listed stream at, and where a stream moved past its place the poll reads on from there and tells each `message.start` and `message.complete` it finds after it, once (`Gateway::take_turns`). The follower tells core each as the very fact this Mac's hooks give (`Turn::hook_event`, through `hooks::Told` and core's hook events), under the far session's id. **Limits, by design:** no `finished` for a turn that ended while the machine was out of reach or the HUD was closed, for a session first seen with the turn already over, for a session that left the live list before its end was read, after the stream's epoch changed, or where Hermes no longer keeps the completion event; a stream that moved is never taken as evidence. **The time on a far completion is when the poll that read it came back: when it was seen, not when the turn ended.** Hermes's events carry no end time, and nothing here says how long before it was seen a turn ended. No card decision reads the places. Client-neutral in core: the alert tracker's decided identities are saved in the data folder (`sound-alerts.json`) and loaded at start, less the ones older than a day, so a request decided before a relaunch does not sound after it however late its card comes back; that holds for every client. A row only hooks made carries the `machine` its fact names. | Stale poll replies | **A poll reply carries the link it was read over, and is dropped once that link is lost or replaced.** Under load, a poll whose replies were read before a link was lost could reach the model after an answer that could not be sent had marked the card as out of reach. The late poll took the mark off, so the card looked answerable on the dead link until the polls' socket failed (ticket 56 saw 13 ms; an answer sent then failed as undelivered and none crossed links). An older reading overrode a newer state. Now: (1) **The route names its link.** `Route::epoch()` is the link the machine is reached over: the route number and, under it, the number the link's keeper gave it; none while there is no link. The SSH route counts a link whose master has exited as none, before its keeper has said so. The route to this Mac needs no link and is one epoch for its whole life, so nothing changes there. (2) **Every socket keeps the link its stream was dialled over** (`Socket::on`). A stream dialled while the link changed is of no one link and is dropped with nothing said on it. (3) **A reading is held against the link that stands, in the locked step that would take it** (`polls.rs`, `stands`). That goes for a poll and for a reading of one session apart. Where the link differs, the reading is dropped whole: the polls' socket is given up as lost (`Input::Lost`, then a new dial), and a lane opens its socket again. The mark then goes as ticket 27 says, by a poll read over a link that stands. **Not covered:** a link that is dead while its master still runs and its keeper has not noticed (a network drop). The route still names it, so a late poll can take the mark off until its socket fails, as before. | Hermes far history | **A far machine's stored sessions and their usage are listed and counted.** Each far machine has a refresh (`platform/src/clients/hermes/history.rs`), made with the machine and the machine's until it is retired, that keeps its mirrors in step with its Hermes databases through ticket 36's reader and ticket 34's one mirror writer, on a thread apart from the polls. Only a session whose fingerprint differs from the one it was written with is fetched. Every fourth read of a tick is the whole sweep's however full the first queue is, and a sweep that last finished over 24 hours ago, or never, goes first until it is done. A session's row and all its usage rows are one mirror transaction marked durable; a row the live list made is replaced in place by `UPDATE` and no session row is ever deleted; a batch read before what the mirror holds, under a route number that is the machine's no more, or for a retired machine is dropped; the live counts' table is never touched. The ledger's only input for a far session stays the far database's own rows, read by the same reader as this Mac's, so each is counted once by model, provider, base URL, billing mode and task. The machines list shows a connected far machine's last contact and its last whole history check as two times, and says where a machine has no history reader. A tick every 60 s while a far session is in a turn or a sweep is overdue, every 5 minutes otherwise (ticket 61: sessions that are only listed, idle, do not count), chosen from a measurement on the Mac Mini: a read takes a quarter of a second whatever its kind and the database's size (2.99 GB), a first sync of 2,504 sessions took 299 reads, and the polls beside them stayed at 18 ms. | Phase 2 design, section 14: ended far sessions and usage split by model can come only from the far database (ticket 37). An entry that leads to nothing the shell can see is asked about with `LC_ALL=C ls -d <entry>/.`, because `sh` cannot say why: only `No such file or directory` is word that it is gone, and it prints nothing; any other reason (`Permission denied`, a link loop, a path through a file) is `unresolved`. The command prints a name as the bytes it is, with no encoding step: a file name holds no NUL, so NUL-ended words carry any name, and `/bin/sh` and `dash` pass the bytes through unchanged. A word that is not UTF-8 is no word of the command's and no profile's name. A profile whose home is at a path that is not UTF-8 cannot be asked and is among the unresolved; a root at such a path is not covered (`root_home_not_canonical`). It is in no ledger table, statistic, cost or quota, and the session as statistics name it keeps the ledger's number. The reason is that the ledger adds running totals by record key and never takes one back, so a count from the gateway followed by the far database's rows under other keys would be counted twice. Counts are committed at most once in 2 s for each machine, the newest of each session, with one word to core, so a far session that works asks core for a pass no more often than a session on this Mac does. A gateway of this Mac is never asked. Chosen over the `session.usage` events the poll already reads, which cost no call but exist only for sessions started over the local socket and trail the count. | Hermes far live counts, their own lane; a grown mirror's changes | **Corrects the row "Hermes far live counts" (ticket 35) in three places and the row "Hermes far rows, grown mirrors and failed reads" (ticket 54) in one.** (1) **Count calls are no step of a poll.** `session.stats` went out in the poll's round, on the polls' socket, and a gateway answers the calls of one socket in turn: with three sessions that work and each call held 2 s, polls came 6 s apart and six cards in ten missed their 4 s bound. The calls are now a round of their own (`gateway::Counting`), run on a lane: a thread and a socket of their own (`platform/src/clients/hermes/counting.rs`). One round follows each poll that came back with somebody to ask, one at a time, and the lane rests after a round for as long as the round took. A call may take as long as any call may (5 s); one that takes longer is given up with the lane's socket. Its socket is closed after 30 s with no round wanted. Polls and cards wait on no count. (2) **A session's last count is asked until it is answered.** It was asked once, when a session that started or worked was listed as something else, and never again if that call failed. A session is now owed a count (`gateway/sample.rs`, `Owed`) when it started, worked **or waited** at one poll and is listed as something else at the next, and when a turn was read to start or end in it (ticket 33's looks), which is how a turn that ran wholly between two idle polls is known of. It stays owed until a call for it is answered, five rounds at most. (3) **A new session's row and its first count are two commits**, where they could be one: the count arrives after the list that made the row. (4) **A mirror's changed rows are all read in the pass their write asks for** (`agents/src/hermes/transcript.rs`): ticket 54 looked at 10,000 of a mirror's old rows a page and read a hundred of those that changed, so a new label took a pass for each hundred rows, two seconds apart (11 passes at 1,000 rows, 51 at 5,000), and reached the newest rows last. A page of a mirror now reads as many as it looks at. A count that moves already took one pass after ticket 54; that is now held by tests at both sizes. Hermes's own database is read a hundred changed sessions a page, as before. Also: `Listing` no longer carries samples (`Writer::counted` takes them), and a line of `core/src/sessions.rs` that ticket 35 changed to no effect is as upstream has it. A session that just asked is so read whole before either goes out, and never queues behind one on a socket that is answered in turn. A quiet session is among the first again the moment the list says something else of it, and for as long as a card of it shows. `Poll::last` says when a poll has reached those calls. (2) **The wait** (`platform/src/clients/hermes/waits.rs`, new; `Pace::still`, 2 s). What a poll asks last is waited for 2 s, all of it together, from when the first of it is asked. What has not answered by then is left as a call never answered always was: a look's session rests 30 s, a session's read goes to a lane, its cards stay as they are, and the next poll asks again. A snapshot of a session that is asked last gets what is left of that time. Every other call keeps its 5 s. Ten seconds, two, and the one second the design gives a poll for its own work: thirteen. A session read apart was read back to back, or every 0.8 s, whatever its gateway's pace: ten reads of 3 s each in 30 s on a gateway polled three times. A lane now reads no more often than the polls come (`every`: the close watch while the model keeps it, the model's own wait otherwise), is kept for six polls at that same pace where it was kept for six close-watch polls (so it could end between two slow polls), and looks every 250 ms whether the pace has quickened. **Corrects** the row "Hermes far alerts" (ticket 33) in one place: the looks went out in the round of the cards' first reads, after them; they now go out after the cards' last reads. **Corrects** "A session read apart" (ticket 30): a lane's pace was `Pace::watch`. **Unchanged:** the model's paces (1 s and 10 s) and when a poll is due (from the start of the poll before, as `link.rs` has paced since ticket 13, which is why timing the idle poll from the last one's start was no fix); the 5 s of every call a new card may wait on; a quiet session is still read at every poll; this Mac's gateways, whose polls ask nothing last. **Not covered:** a session whose own read or whose profile's list is slow still waits for it, as at every pace, and a card of a quiet session that is itself slow to read shows a slow pace and one read of its own later. | Enter on a question card | **Enter submits a question card the way its Submit (or Next) button does, while the HUD has the keyboard.** A click on one of a question card's offered answers now takes the keyboard too, as a text field or a selection already did, so the next Enter reaches the card; a press dragged off the answer takes nothing. A click that clears or repeats an answer takes it as well, which is accepted. It ends when the pointer leaves the HUD, the window loses focus, the card goes, or a press lands anywhere else. Enter does nothing while Submit is off (nothing chosen), during input-method composition (`isComposing` or keyCode 229), with Shift, Alt, Ctrl or ⌘, or on a press a field already handled (the typed answer's own Enter), so one press submits once. A held Enter's repeats (Enter or keypad Enter) are stopped by one capture listener before any control sees them while a request card is on show, so no button, answer or box is pressed or toggled again; the first press works as before. Two separate deliberate presses send what two clicks on Submit always have: Core drops a waiting request once it takes a decision. Only the card the stack shows open takes it (where several request cards are drawn, the last, drawn over the others); with a permission card in front, no question behind it is answered, and an answer chosen on a card behind submits nothing. A focused Back, Skip, Submit or link is left to the browser, as is Enter on an answer reached with Tab while Submit is off: the browser presses that answer, so it is chosen, and the next Enter submits. There is no multi-line field, so Shift+Enter has nothing to keep. **Permission and plan cards get nothing new:** none of their buttons is a safe default, so Enter on the card does nothing, and every approving button (Allow once, Always allow, each choice Core names, Approve, and Approve, accept edits) swallows Enter even when focused, so Enter never approves; Space and a click still do. Deny keeps Enter. | Approve automatically, held to the fork's rules | Upstream 0.6.7 adds Approve automatically (Settings → Agents, off by default): a permission request from a client it is on for is allowed at once, with no card. The fork holds it to four rules, the first three in `core/src/requests/auto_limits.rs`, each tested. **(1) It never decides a request marked `unreachable`.** A native card that says its client cannot be reached is registered and waits, as ticket 27's gate has it for every other decision (`approve_automatically_never_decides_a_request_marked_unreachable`). **(2) It never answers a Hermes request.** A channel descriptor says whether its requests always wait for the person (`Channel::answered_by_hand`, new, client-neutral; true for Hermes's gateway only), and upstream's rule asks it first, so neither a native card nor a hook's callback is approved (`approve_automatically_never_answers_a_hermes_request`, `only_hermes_is_answered_by_hand`, and through the real connection `tests_unix::approvals::approve_automatically_never_answers_a_hermes_approval`: the card shows and Hermes is sent nothing until a button is pressed). Settings offers no "Approve automatically" switch on Hermes's page and never tags it "Auto-approve": Platform marks such a client `by-hand` in `ClientRow.features` (`client_features.rs`, `approve-by-hand.ts`). **(3) What is known to be a request it allowed plays no sound, and nothing else is silent.** While the client takes the answer, its channel can still tell the session as waiting, and a wait with no card makes a `needs-you`. Core keeps each approved request under its own names: its id, and the tool call it names (from `register_native`, and from the hook callback through `Requests::note_auto_approved`). A native channel can say which requests a session's wait is for, by the ids it registered them under (`Requests::set_native_waits`, new, client-neutral). Codex Desktop's channel does, and only where the wait is known to be those requests' and nothing else's (`desktop/waits.rs`): it reads the names out of each state's own requests, never out of an earlier state's, and names none where the requests could not be read, where the thread also waits on the person's input, or where the state lists a request waiting in the thread that the channel shows no card for (a connector it does not know, a shape it cannot read). A request that is done, or another thread's, does not count. The sound decision is silent for a wait with no card only where its channel names requests for it and every one of them was allowed automatically, and for a transcript sighting of an approved tool call. **A wait that names no request always sounds**, as before the sync, though a request was just approved in its session: an extra sound for an approved request is the lesser fault, and a silent wait is not. So the wait a hook reports after an automatic approval sounds (a hook's wait names nothing), and so does the wait of any channel that does not name its requests. **A request is approved, for sound, only once its channel confirmed that the automatic answer was delivered** (round 6). Core keeps each request it allowed and says it is approved only from the channel's own confirmation on: a native channel's word that the very answer it sent was delivered, told by the number of the wait that answer was given in (`Requests::auto_answer_delivered`, round 8; a ticket's `wait`, which core never uses twice), or, for a hook, its reply written whole to the hook that asked (`hooks/callback/answer.rs`). The Windows pipe writes the reply after the handler returns and does not say whether it was written, so the Windows listener never says the hook took the reply (`answer.deliver(&requests, |_| false)`, rounds 7 and 8): a hook's automatic Allow is never told to core as approved there, and what it leaves sounds. `Answer::deliver` is the one way out of an answer for every listener, so that no part of it is unused in the Windows library, where warnings are errors (`hooks/callback/windows_shape_tests.rs` compiles the file a second time with only the Windows calls and dead code an error). Until then the request is not approved, and its wait and its card sound as they did before the sync: while the answer is queued or on its way, where it failed or nobody confirmed it (`failed`, `uncertain`), where its listener stopped before it left (settling a request ends its approval, `Requests::settle_native`), and where nothing was heard of it in ten minutes: a first confirmation that comes later confirms nothing, whether or not a look came in between (the word itself is held to the age, round 7). **Word of an answer speaks for that answer alone** (round 8): a receipt as the panel shows it names a request by its id, and Codex Desktop can ask again under the id it used before, so a receipt by id says nothing to the sound decision, either way. The first answer's confirmation, coming late, confirms nothing of the request that took its id; only Codex Desktop's channel tells core by the wait (`desktop/reply.rs`), and it is the only native channel whose waits can be silent at all, since only it names the requests a wait is for. **What is known of an automatic answer does not outlast the stream it was known on** (round 9). Where Codex Desktop's channel loses sight of a thread's requests (its stream drops, the thread becomes unknown to it, or a state comes whose requests it cannot read), it cannot tell what became of them, and what it sees next under the same id may be another request. So core drops the approval, confirmed or not, and the dismissal that kept the id from being registered again (`Requests::auto_approval_lost`; `desktop.rs`, `desktop/cards.rs`). Seen again, the request is registered as new, answered anew, and approved only once that answer is confirmed; until then it sounds. A request that was answered by hand keeps its dismissal, as before the sync. The cost: a request that is still the same one after a short drop is answered a second time, which its owner refuses, and it sounds once. Stopping the listener already settled every request. Antigravity's and DeepSeek's channels name no requests and tell core of no answer by its wait, so nothing of theirs is ever approved for sound (`requests::auto_limits::tests::a_channel_that_never_tells_core_by_the_wait_has_nothing_approved`). The sound decision asks core at every look which requests are approved (`Requests::auto_approved`, the confirmed ones only) and remembers no approval from the look before: where core says so no longer, the wait sounds, whenever that look comes. A card always sounds, whatever was approved under its name. **A request that waits with no card and is not approved is a wait of its own** (round 7): where its channel names it, it sounds once under the name its card would have (`needs-you:<source>:<session>:<its id>`), whatever the session's wait sounded for before, so a second approval that takes the place of the first with no frame between that says the thread is at work sounds too, as its card did before the sync. Its own card, if it comes, goes by that same name and does not sound again; a card for another request does. Seen to end and named again later, it is another wait and goes by the name after it, as a wait that names nothing does (round 9, `sound_alerts/bare.rs`): seen to end means a look at which the session waits no more, or its wait names the request no more. A request that shows its card is told by the card exactly as before the sync. One cost, accepted as an extra sound: a look that lands after the answer left and before its confirmation pings once for a request that is then confirmed (`codex::desktop::tests::auto_answer::a_look_while_the_automatic_answer_is_on_its_way_sounds_once`); how often the real app looks in that gap is not measured. No card comes back for a failed answer by itself, as none did before the sync for an answered card whose answer failed; the receipt shows as it did. Nothing is inferred from the order of looks or from a clock. A confirmed request is kept until a look sees the wait that names it end; the look tells core which ids those were, with their session, and core ends those there and no other, so a request approved while a look was deciding is kept, and so is one of another source or session that goes by the same id, and so is one registered anew under that id whose answer is not yet confirmed (round 9). Where no look sees its wait end, it is kept ten minutes (`APPROVED_KEPT_MS`, the longest a request waits for an answer by default), and past that only while its channel still says a wait is for it: a confirmed answer's wait stays silent however long it lasts, by what core says at each look. A hook's approval has no such wait, so a transcript sighting of its call that is still there after ten minutes sounds, once. At most 512 are kept (`APPROVED_KEPT`), the oldest going first. **A wait with no card that is seen to end and begins again sounds again.** A channel's waiting flag has no time of its own, so such a wait is known by the time of the session's last event, and a second wait with nothing written in between went by the first one's name: decided already, and silent. The host also plays an alert once by its name, so the same name told again would not be heard. A borrowed name is now marked as over when its wait is seen to end, or when the approved requests' wait takes its place, and the next wait goes by the name after it (`sound_alerts/bare.rs`, `sound_alerts/tests/again.rs`). A wait known by a time of its own, a hook's attention, is one wait however often it is seen, as before. This changes one thing from before the sync, on purpose: a channel's wait that ends and begins again with nothing written between now sounds twice, where it sounded once. One known cost of it, accepted as an extra sound: where Codex Desktop sends one frame whose runtime flags are missing (`activeFlags` null) between two frames that say the thread waits on the same request, the session is seen at work for that frame, and the same request sounds a second time (the round 4 verifier's probe; before the sync it sounded once, `evidence/66/round5/classification.txt`, finding 3). The frame says the thread waits on nothing, and keeping the wait across it would be a guess that could leave a new wait silent. Tests: through the whole core `tests::auto_approved_sound::*` and, for round 6, `tests::auto_confirmed_sound::*` (the round 5 verifier's two probes by name: a failed answer has sounded whenever the next look comes, at 599,999, 600,000 and 900,000 ms; a request settled before its answer left sounds when its card shows; and a confirmed answer's wait stays silent past ten minutes), with the round 6 verifier's expiry probe (`a_first_confirmation_after_ten_minutes_confirms_nothing`), the round 7 verifier's reuse of an id (`word_of_an_earlier_answer_confirms_nothing_of_a_request_that_reuses_its_id`) and each of the earlier verifiers' scenarios by name (a wait first seen 5,001 ms and a minute late is silent; a separate wait after a look sounds; a distinct wait that names no request sounds before the first look, once; a wait seen before its request was approved has sounded once; a look between an approval and the publication of its wait leaves it silent; an approval made during a look is kept); the decision alone, `sound_alerts::tests::approved::*`; the record, `requests::auto_limits::tests::*`; the channel, `codex::desktop::waits::tests::*` (among them the listener that stopped before its queued answer left, reopened with the switch off, and the round 6 verifier's two approvals one after the other, `a_second_approval_that_takes_the_place_of_the_first_sounds_too`, with the switch on and off) and `codex::desktop::tests::receipt_reuse::*` (the round 7 verifier's five replays through the channel's real answer sockets, each with the switch on and off), `codex::desktop::tests::reconnect::*` (the round 8 verifier's dropped stream on the real sockets, with and without a look at the first wait) and `codex::desktop::waits::tests::a_request_seen_again_after_the_channel_lost_sight_of_it_sounds_and_is_answered_anew` (all three ways of losing sight) and `codex::desktop::tests::auto_answer::*`; the hook's reply, `hooks::callback::tests::an_approved_request_is_noted_for_the_sound_decision_once_its_reply_is_delivered` and `hooks::transport::delivered_tests::*`, over the real socket and, for the Windows hand-over, replayed on a socket pair whose other end has closed. The first two versions of this rule guessed which wait was the approved request's, first by a five-second window and then by the order of looks, and ticket 66's verifiers showed each guess both pinging for the approved request and muting another wait. Rounds 3 to 5 took a request as approved from the moment it was allowed, and the verifiers then found waits left silent where the answer never arrived: after ten minutes, and after a listener stopped. Round 6 turned that around, so that only a confirmed answer is silent. Round 7 closed three ways around it that its verifier found: a second request hidden behind the first one's sound, a confirmation that came after the ten minutes, and the Windows hand-over. Round 8 closed a fourth, a receipt for one answer confirming another request of the same id, and undid two faults of round 7's own: a receipt's `sessionId` in the snapshot had become core's `source:session` key, which hid the panel's notice of a failed answer under an open card of that session (`tests::delivery_notice`, and the HUD's `test/request-delivery.test.ts`, both from `core/tests/fixtures/request-delivery-notice.json`), and the Windows library had dead code. `requests/deliveries.rs` carries no line of this ticket's any more. Two of their cases look the same to the sound decision and need opposite answers, which is why it no longer guesses. **(4) It never allows a call the person's own rule says to ask about.** Factory Droid's hook reads the person's command rules, and a call one of them says to ask about is marked (`RULE_ASKS`) so that the HUD holds it, as the agent contract says. Upstream's automatic branch looked only at the request's kind and its client, so with the switch on such a call was allowed at once, which ticket 66's verifier showed. The callback now leaves a marked call to its card (`hooks::callback::tests::a_call_the_persons_own_rule_asks_about_is_never_approved_automatically`, through the real policy screen with Factory settings in a home of the test's own: the marked call waits as a card and hears nothing, and an unmarked Droid call beside it is allowed). A rule that blocks, or allows, settles the call before the callback as before. ## Hermes live channel

Design for tickets 12 to 16. The pure half is built (ticket 12), and so is the connection (ticket 13, "The connection, as built" below).

### Shapes

All in `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/`, with no I/O and no clock.

- **Where something happens** (`machine.rs`, `identity.rs`). `Machine` is `This` or `Far(key)`. `machine_key(install_id, root_home)` is the only way to a key: the first 16 hex characters of the SHA-256 of the install id, a line feed and the far root home. It gives no key and a reason (`NoKey`) unless the id is exactly 32 lowercase hex characters, which is what Hermes writes, and the home is written as a canonical path is. Nothing stands in for a missing id, a connection entry's id least of all, and no value holds the raw id, so it is in no id and no `Debug` output. `identities_agree()` says whether every gateway found on one route reports the same install id; a route where they differ leads to no one machine. **The root home rule.** A root home is accepted only in the one form a canonical path has: it starts with `/`, names at least one folder, and has no ending slash, no doubled slash, no `.` or `..` folder and no NUL. `/srv/hermes/`, `/srv//hermes`, `/srv/./hermes`, `/srv/x/../hermes`, `//srv/hermes` and `/` are all refused (`NoKey::Home`), never tidied, so one folder cannot get two keys. The model reads no disk and cannot resolve a link: whoever hands a home over (tickets 29 and 30) resolves it where the home is, as `realpath` does, and hands over that exact text every time. The same rule holds for the root given to `anchor()` and `Profile::in_root()`. `GatewayId` is a machine and an anchor profile, and stays the same across the gateway's restarts. `anchor(root_home, served homes)` picks the anchor: the default profile if the gateway serves the root, else the named profile whose home sorts first, whatever order they are listed in. It is given the root because a home's own path cannot say which profile it is: a root may itself lie in a folder called `profiles` (`/srv/profiles/work`). `Profile::in_root(root, home)` is the default profile at the root, a named one at `<root>/profiles/<name>`, and nothing anywhere else; a served home that is no profile of the root is left out of the choice. A gateway is written `local` (this machine, default anchor; phase 1's one gateway), `local/<anchor>` (another gateway on this machine) or `<key>/<anchor>` (a far one). `Profile` is `Default` or `Named(ProfileName)`. A `ProfileName` can only be made of a name Hermes allows (`hermes_constants.PROFILE_ID_RE`): 1 to 64 of `a-z`, `0-9`, `_` and `-`, starting with a letter or a digit, and never `default`. `Profile::named()` and `Profile::folder()` give none for anything else and tidy nothing, so no profile holds a slash or a colon. `SessionRef` is gateway, profile and the gateway's session id, made by `SessionRef::new()`, which gives none for an id that can be no session's: an empty one, or one with a slash before its first colon. `hud_session_id(machine, profile, id)` is the one function that builds a HUD session id, for `SessionRef::hud_id()`, the hooks and the `state.db` reader, and refuses the same ids: `hermes:<id>` and `hermes:<profile>:<id>` on this machine, `hermes:<key>/<profile>:<id>` on a far one, the profile always written, `default` too. **Why a far id is never a local one.** Between `hermes:` and the next colon, a far id holds a slash (after its key) and an id of this machine never does: a profile's name holds none, and a session id that would put one there is refused. That holds for whatever a hook payload, a `state.db` row, a folder name or a gateway's list hands over: a hook that names a malformed profile or session reports nothing, a `state.db` row with such an id is left out, a listed session with such an id is not followed, and a folder that is no profile's name is never taken for one (below). Every id Hermes itself writes is what it was, byte for byte. The gateway is no part of a session id: two gateways on one machine with the same profile and session id name the same session, and a profile can move between them. Request ids keep their form, `hermes:<gateway>:<profile>:<session>:<asked>`, with the gateway written as above, so two gateways never share one. The reader builds this machine's ids, and a mirror's far ids through `stored_session_id(stored key, profile, id)`, which writes through the same hand, so a stored session and a live one are one id. **A stored key** (`StoredKey`, ticket 23) is a key read back from a file Agent HUD wrote: exactly 16 lowercase hex characters, nothing tidied. It names stored sessions and proves nothing, so there is no way from it to a `MachineKey`, a far `Machine` or a `GatewayId`; only `MachineKey::stored()` goes the other way. **A home always has a profile; only a named value is checked.** `Profile::of_home(home)` is how the hooks (`HERMES_HOME`), the `state.db` reader and `identify()` name a home by its path alone, as phase 1 did: a home whose last two folders are `profiles/<name>`, with a name Hermes allows, is the named profile `<name>`, and every other home is the default profile of the root it is. So `HERMES_HOME` can be any folder (`/srv/profiles/Work`, `/srv/profiles/.hermes`, `/opt/hermes`): it is followed, read and reported, as `hermes:<id>`. The grammar is checked where a profile is named by a value: `Profile::named()`, a hook payload's `profile` field, and `hud_session_id()` through the `Profile` it is given. A payload that names a malformed profile reports nothing, whatever its home. **What is left open.** By its path alone, a custom root that is itself at `/x/profiles/<valid-name>` reads as the named profile `<valid-name>`, not as a root's default profile. That is how the ids already saved in the ledger were made (`hermes:work:s1`), so it is kept: changing it would move saved history to a second namespace. Ticket 26 keeps that everywhere: a gateway's sessions, cards, request ids, waiting flags and live states are named by the home's path alone, exactly as the hooks and the reader name them, so one session is one row (`core/src/tests/hermes_identity.rs`), and every call is routed by that same name. That is also what Hermes does: it takes the folder above `profiles` for the root of such a home, so a home at `/x/profiles/work` is the profile `work` to it, and a call for `default` is refused as `profile_mismatch` (`hermes_constants.py`, `_hermes_root_of`; `hermes_cli/profiles.py`, `get_profile_dir` and `profile_matches_home`; the second PR review of ticket 26 traced it and ran it against a real 0.22.5 gateway, `evidence/26/verifier-files-2/`). `identify()` is not told the root. The root home of the machine, as Agent HUD is configured, picks one thing: the anchor a gateway is named by (`anchor()`), so the gateway that serves the configured root is `local`, as in phase 1. The first two versions of ticket 26 named, and then routed to, such a root as `default`; both PR reviews caught it. **A profile folder ending in a line feed is not a named profile** (ruling on the second review's finding 2). Python's `$` lets Hermes's own pattern match `work\n`, so Hermes would list and serve a folder of that name if one existed. Hermes never creates one: creating a profile strips whitespace. Letting a line feed into session ids and log lines is the greater risk, so the name is refused as a named profile and no code was changed for it. Such a folder falls under the rule above like any other folder that is no profile's name: it is read as a default profile, and no id holds its line feed.
- **Connection state** (`connection.rs`). `Disconnected`, `Connecting`, `Ready(Instance)`, `Unsupported(reason)`, `Failed(reason)`. `identify()` reads the control socket's answer with the checks Hermes's own client applies, and one of its own: an origin that is not plain `http` on this machine is `Unsupported`. A new `instance_id` is a restart.
- **Pending item** (`ask.rs`). `Pending` is a `SessionRef`, an `Ask` and `wait_ms`: how long Hermes said it waits for an answer. Hermes says how long and not since when, so when the wait ends is never known. `wait_ms` is for a card to say. No card closes on a timer of Agent HUD's own. `Ask` is one of three types. `Question` has a prompt id, the turn's `execution_generation`, the text, the choices and whether several may be picked. `Approval` has a prompt id, the generation, the command, the description and only the choices Hermes offered. `Batch` is the set of questions Hermes sends as a request of its own (`clarify`, id `srq-…`) when the terminal UI started the turn. It has a request id and ordered questions, each with what another viewer already answered or skipped, and no generation and no deadline.
- **Decision** (`decision.rs`). `Question::answer(Answer)`, `Approval::respond(Choice)` and `Batch::answer(answers)` are the only ways to build an answer, so an approval's choice cannot be put on a question. A doc test proves it does not compile. `Answer` cannot be empty, and a question is never skipped: Hermes has no skip a viewer can send for one question that holds on every runtime. `Batch::answer` always settles the set: questions not answered here and not locked by another viewer go as `null` (skipped). `Pending::reply(decision)` turns the HUD's `RequestDecision` into the exact frame. `allow` is `once`, `deny` is `deny`, `always` is `always`, and `always` with `suggestion: {type: "scope", scope: "session"}` is `session`.
- **Card** (`card.rs`). `Pending::view(now, machine)` is the usual request view; a far machine's label, when given, is its `machine`, and without one the card has no `machine` at all. An approval lists the lasting choices Hermes offers in `suggestions`, so a limited request shows once and deny alone. Each question of a set has `lock`: null, `{state: "answered", answer}` or `{state: "skipped"}`.
- **State** (`state.rs`, `poll.rs`, `fold.rs`, `reconcile.rs`, `effect.rs`). `Gateway::poll()` begins a poll. The `Poll` says which calls to make (`steps()`) and takes their results (`answer`, `failed`). `Gateway::apply(Input, now_ms) -> Vec<Effect>` takes the finished poll and everything else that happened: identify answered, the socket was lost, an answer was sent or replied to. Effects are what to do: open a card, change it, close it with the reason, show it again, poll now.

A card closes when a poll finds its request settled, its turn over, its set of questions no longer open, or its session gone from the live list; when an answer to it comes back `resolved`, `already_resolved` or `stale_generation`; and when the gateway restarts or turns out not to have the live connection. A dropped socket closes nothing: the request still waits in Hermes. Time passing closes nothing.

### How the cards follow Hermes

The cards follow polls. A poll asks Hermes what waits now, and the model opens, changes and closes cards until they match. Nothing read in one poll is carried into the next. The first two state layers treated every reading as an event to merge into remembered state, and every order of delivery became a bug (`evidence/12/verifier-report.md`, `verifier-report-2.md`). This one has nothing to merge.

- **What is kept between polls.** The open cards, each keyed by gateway, profile, session and the gateway's own request id. The gateway's instance id. A count of polls. When a snapshot was last taken of each waiting session (see below); that is the one thing a poll leaves for the next beside the cards. Also what the last poll listed each session as doing, which is only reported (`statuses()`, `poll_ms()`) and never read by a poll. No cursors, no epochs, no record of what was over.
- **One poll.** `session.active_list` for every served profile. Then each session that works or waits or shows a card is read with `session.events.since`: once without an epoch, which Hermes always answers empty with the stream's epoch and latest event, and then for the events. Each reply also lists the sets of questions open now (`open_requests`). None of this subscribes. Sessions that are idle or starting and show no card are not read.
- **The fold** (`fold.rs`). From one gap-free run of events in the gateway's order: a request waits from its request event until an event says it settled. A `message.complete` takes every request of its turn and of earlier turns, wherever the request event stands in the run. A later turn's `message.start` takes the requests of earlier turns. A session that runs no turn (listed idle or starting) keeps no card. The sets of questions are whatever `open_requests` lists. A question or an approval that Agent HUD cannot show (a kind Hermes adds later) gets no card, and no snapshot either.
- **The poll reads from the oldest event Hermes still keeps.** Hermes keeps the last 512 events and 4 MiB of a session's stream. It answers a read whole if it starts no earlier than the last event it dropped, and refuses it otherwise (`tui_gateway/event_replay.py`, `is_truncated`; `gateway/session_events.py`, `since`). One short turn is about 24 events, so a read from 0 stops working a few turns in and never works again for that stream. The refusal says where the stream ends and not where it begins, so the poll searches for the oldest event it can read (`read.rs`). It first reads from where that is if only the count made Hermes drop anything: 512 before the latest, or 0. That is usually right, for 2 calls in all. If Hermes refuses (it also drops by size), the poll asks whether the very last event is kept and then halves the range between what was refused and what was answered. On a stream that stands still the search ends on the last event dropped, in at most 12 calls, and every request whose event can be read is seen.
- **A search that does not finish.** A session gets at most 16 calls in one poll. A stream that keeps moving under the search can use them up before the oldest event is found. The poll then knows it has not read everything. It closes no question or approval card of that session on what it did read, opens what it found, and takes a snapshot if one is due.
- **What no read can show: a snapshot.** Hermes lists a session as `waiting` exactly while a question or an approval waits in it (`gateway/session_active_list.py`, `_status`). A request asked before the oldest event read shows in no read, whatever else is seen waiting. Only a snapshot lists it: `session.resume` on a socket of its own, closed after the reply. Its `prompts` and `open_requests` are what waits. A session that does not wait is never snapshotted: no question or approval waits there. A waiting session is unsure, and a snapshot is wanted, when any of these holds: Hermes has dropped events of its stream; the search did not finish; nothing in its events is waited on; a card shows whose request and whose end are both missing from the events.
- **When a wanted snapshot is taken.** At once, if the model has no record of one for that session. Otherwise 30 s after the last (`SNAPSHOT_EVERY_MS`), counted from the time of the poll before. The record is dropped, so that the next poll takes one at once, when a poll lists the session as not waiting, when any card of the session closes (answered here or seen over by a poll), when the socket is lost, and when the gateway restarts. A snapshot that fails leaves no record. Between snapshots, a card the events do not speak for stays as it is.
- **What that costs.** A session whose stream has dropped events, which is any session past 512 events, takes one snapshot when it starts to wait, one after each of its cards closes while it still waits, and one every 30 s for as long as it goes on waiting. A session that has dropped nothing takes none.
- **What a snapshot does to the session.** `session.resume` subscribes. It returns the whole message history, cancels a pending close of the session, lets its background work start again, and keeps the session alive while that socket is open (ticket 11, answer 2). On an idle session it also re-commits the session's settings. A snapshot is only asked for of a session listed as waiting, which is busy and so keeps its settings. But the list is a moment earlier than the snapshot: if the request settles and the turn ends in between, the session is idle when `session.resume` runs and its settings are re-committed. The same holds for the short socket an answer is sent on.
- **Stale polls.** A poll carries the count it was begun with. The count moves when a poll begins, when an answer comes back `resolved`, `already_resolved` or `stale_generation`, when the socket is lost and when the gateway restarts. A poll whose count is not the current one when it comes back is dropped whole. So a poll that read "still waiting" just before an answer landed cannot reopen the card, and a poll of a gateway that has restarted since cannot fill it again.
- **What a poll could not tell.** If a profile's list fails, or a session's read or snapshot fails, the cards concerned stay as they are and the next poll asks everything again. There is no retry state to get stuck in.
- **Approvals settle late.** When nobody answers an approval, Hermes stops waiting but publishes `approval.settled` only when something next asks what waits, or at the end of the turn (ticket 11, answer 3). The card stays until a poll sees that, the turn ends, or an answer given on the card comes back `stale_generation` or `already_resolved`. From source, the poll's own `session.active_list` makes Hermes publish it sooner (`_status` calls `PendingControls.snapshot`, which settles what is no longer active). That was not measured.
- **Deadlines.** `timeout_s` is a length with no start. The model keeps it as `wait_ms` for a card to say and never closes a card on it.
- **Sets of questions.** A poll that lists a set already shown, with different answers from other viewers, changes the card in place (`Effect::Changed`). Nothing replies to the answer to a set, so a set still listed 5 s after its answer was written is shown again (`Effect::Unanswered`); that closes nothing.
- **What can still be missed, and for how long.** A request that waits and that no read shows gets its card at the next snapshot of its session, so it can stay hidden for up to 30 s plus one poll. Three ways it happens. A missed change: the session stopped waiting and started again between two polls, with nothing to close and no lost socket to say so, and the new request's event is already dropped. The whole ring dropped: Hermes keeps 64 streams and 64 MiB across all sessions, so other sessions' activity can drop this one's events with no burst of its own. A moving stream: the search ran out of calls before the oldest event. The same bound holds the other way: a card whose request ended with no event left to say so, in a session that still waits on something else, stays up to 30 s.
- **Cost.** Folding a full stream (512 events, 4.2 MB as JSON) and settling the cards takes about 0.07 ms in a release build and under 1 ms in a debug build; parsing that reply takes 1 ms and about 25 ms (`tests/cost.rs`, MacBook, under load). So each poll reads again everything Hermes keeps of each busy session, and nothing is read incrementally. This measures the model's part only. It does not measure Hermes serving the calls, the extra calls of a search (up to twice the stream's bytes when Hermes dropped by size), or a snapshot, which returns the session's whole history and comes as often as "What that costs" says.

### Shell hooks: kept

The hooks stay installed for running and finished. They are pushes that already work and are tested, and they are all there is for a gateway without the live connection (the Mini's 0.21.6 today). The live connection adds what hooks cannot: the text of a question and a way to answer. Holding a subscription on every session to replace the hooks would change how Nicky's sessions end and re-commit idle sessions' settings (ticket 11, answer 2).

### What ticket 13 must implement

The connection goes in `src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/`, started from `hooks::configure` when `clients::runs(&agents::hermes::GATEWAY)`. It owns one `Gateway` per gateway (`GatewayId::new(Machine::This, Profile::Default)` in phase 1, written `local`), feeds it every input and carries out every effect. It decides nothing else.

1. **Find the control socket from a Hermes home passed in.** `<home>/gateway.sock` (`CONTROL_SOCKET`). If that is missing, read `<home>/gateway.sock.path` (`CONTROL_POINTER`) and pass its text to `pointed_socket()`. Check that the home, the pointer and the socket are the user's own with no access for others, and that the listener is the same user. Production passes the resolved `HERMES_HOME`, else `~/.hermes`. Tests pass an isolated home with its own gateway. Never test against `~/.hermes`: adding a profile there rewrites Nicky's `config.yaml` (ticket 11, deviation 1). Resolve links in the home before calling `identify(answer, home)`, which compares it as written.
2. **Handshake.** Send `identify_line()`, one JSON line in and one out, and give the answer to the model as `Input::Identified`. Retry once on a timeout before `Input::Lost(Unreachable)`. On `Ready`, send `ticket_line(instance, Scope::Host)` (it has no `subject`), read the answer with `granted()`, and dial `ws://` and `SOCKET_PATH` under `instance.origin` with `ticket.subprotocols()`, no `Origin` header and no proxy. `identify()` has checked that the origin is plain `http` on this machine, IPv4 or IPv6. Any other origin (`https`, another host) is `Unsupported(Origin)`: it is left to the hooks, no ticket is asked for and nothing dials. There is no TLS. Phase 1 is the local gateway, which serves plain http, and phase 2 reaches a far gateway through a port of this machine forwarded over SSH, which is plain http too. Check the echoed subprotocol is `SUBPROTOCOL`. A refusal goes in as `Input::Denied`, HTTP 403 as `Input::Lost(TicketRefused)`. Allow frames up to 8 MiB.
3. **Tickets are never kept.** Mint, dial, drop. A ticket lasts 30 s and opens one socket. `Ticket` prints as `<masked>`; do not log the subprotocol list.
4. **Wait, do not fall back, while a gateway starts.** `Connecting` after `Identified::Wait` means ask again with backoff. `Unsupported` means stop and leave Hermes to the hooks; ask again rarely, since an update can add the connection. A draining gateway that waits for its turns (`instance.draining`) is `Ready` and can be answered.
5. **Run polls, one at a time for each gateway.** `gateway.poll()` begins one (None unless the gateway is ready). Ask `poll.steps()`, send each as `step.command(Scope::Host).frame(id)`, and give each reply's `result` to `poll.answer(&step, result)`. Call `poll.failed(&step)` for an error reply, a timeout, or a profile that answers `profile_mismatch` or `runtime_draining`. Ask `steps()` again until it is empty, then `gateway.apply(Input::Polled(poll), now)`. The steps of one round do not depend on each other and may be sent together. Never begin a second poll of a gateway before the first was given back or dropped: the model drops the older one, and its calls were wasted. If the socket is lost during a poll, drop the `Poll` and send `Input::Lost`. A poll covers every profile in `instance.served`; a host's list call covers one profile, which is why there is one `Step::List` each.
6. **`Step::Snapshot` goes on a socket of its own.** New ticket, new socket, `session.resume`, read the reply, close the socket. Closing ends the subscription. Never keep it open and never send `end_reason`. The reply holds the session's whole history: allow 8 MiB frames. If it fails, call `poll.failed(&step)`: the model takes it again at the next poll. Give every `apply` the real time in milliseconds: the model spaces a session's snapshots 30 s apart by it. What a snapshot does to the session, and how often one comes, is under "How the cards follow Hermes".
7. **When to poll.** A timer that never stops: `gateway.poll_ms()` after each poll ends, which is 1 s (`BUSY_POLL_MS`) while any session starts, works or waits or a card shows, and 10 s (`IDLE_POLL_MS`) when everything looks idle. The slow poll is what finds a turn that no hook and no hint told of. Poll at once, as one "poll wanted" flag however many ask, on `Effect::Poll`, on a frame for which `hints(frame)` is true on any open socket (Hermes's `sessions.changed` and `platforms.changed`, which it sends at most every 2 s and can miss), when a Hermes hook reports a turn started, waiting or ended, at startup and after every reconnect. A wanted poll starts when the one under way ends. Hints and hooks only say when to poll. What waits is always read by the poll.
8. **Answer on a short socket.** Take the decision with `Requests::checked`, build `pending.reply(decision, scope)`. `Ok(None)` is leave it. For `Reply::Call`: new ticket, new socket, `session.resume` (an answer from a socket that is not subscribed is refused), send `call.frame(id)`, tell the model `Input::Sent`, give it the reply as `Input::Responded`, close the socket. For `Reply::Result` (a set of questions): resume, write the frame, `Input::Sent`, close. Nothing replies to it, so the model asks for a poll at once, closes the card when a poll no longer lists the set, and says `Effect::Unanswered` if it is still listed 5 s later.
9. **Effects to core.** `Open(p)` is `register_native(p.id(), p.view(now), now)`. `Changed(p)` is `refresh_native(p.id(), p.view(now))`, never a second request. `Close { id, session, .. }` is `settle_native(id)`; `session` is the full `SessionRef`, gateway included, and `session.hud_id()` is the HUD's session id. `Unanswered { pending, why }` gives the card back as it was (`Requests::put_back`, since ticket 27; it was `settle_native` then `register_native` again, which started its hold anew), plus a failed delivery; after `PermissionDenied`, resume and send again. `Marked(p)` and `Unmarked(p)` are `refresh_native` with the card as it is now (ticket 27). `Poll` sets the "poll wanted" flag. After any change call `set_native_waiting(AGENT.id, reader, &sessions, now)` with the sessions that have cards.
