The reader looks at the column names of both tables first, in the list that holds generated and hidden columns too (`pragma_table_xinfo`; the plain list leaves them out, which the fourth review found), which loads no row, and answers `db-rowid`: `Far::Rowid`, "state.db declares a column that hides its row numbers". Hermes's schema declares none (third review, finding 2; fourth review).
- **For ticket 37: a paged read by rowid passes over rows that move below its cursor.** Hermes's `sessions` table keeps its rowids through a VACUUM, because its text key gives it an index. A table with no index at all, which the reader accepts, is renumbered by one, and a row can be inserted below the cursor between two reads. Either way a row that lands below the cursor is not read in that sweep, and the next whole sweep reads it. No healthy row is marked skipped by it. This is true of any paged read by rowid (fourth review, a note).
- **No page is asked from a rowid below zero.** The caller refuses to send one, the far program refuses one from anyone, and the checker takes no number below zero in any answer's last line (second review, finding 3).
- **The reader never runs Python's own shutdown (ticket 55).** Every end it makes is `os._exit`: 0 after the last line is written, 1 from the crash handler, 3 and 5 from the watchdog, 4 from a failed write, the crash line's too (ticket 59). The deadline's system timer, behind the watchdog, ends it by its signal. The one way left through the shutdown is a second interrupt that comes while the crash line is written. A change that lets it end another way (a `return` past the last line, `sys.exit`) brings back a rare crash of Python 3.9, whose watchdog thread can wake as the shutdown frees its state. So nothing may be left for the shutdown to do: no buffered output (`print`, `sys.stdout`), no exit hook.
- **Statuses.** 0: it said what it had to (an answer, "busy", "too large", "no database", "sqlite-old"). 1: it crashed, with one line on standard error that holds no row text. 3: the deadline. 4: its channel was gone. 5: too much memory. 127: the far shell found no `python3`, which with a Python that has no `sqlite3` module is `Failure::NoHistory`, "no history on this machine".
- **Every number of an answer's last line is checked, for every kind of answer.** A probe and the held file count nothing; a sessions read counts sessions and usage rows and no page; a page of fingerprints counts fingerprints, the rows looked at and the last rowid, which is past where the page began by at least one for each row (finding 3).
- **Nothing is logged but kinds, counts, times and statuses.** The log sink given to `Reader::new` gets one line for each read and each wait. Session ids are not logged.
- **A far profile whose gateway is stopped shows no history until that gateway runs again, where the far Python has Apple's SQLite.** A database in write-ahead mode has no `-wal` and no `-shm` file once Hermes has closed it. The reader opens a database one way only, through a connection that cannot write (`mode=ro`), and never as a database that cannot change, which the design rules out. Apple's SQLite (`/usr/bin/python3`) will not open such a database that way (measured: "unable to open database file", and no file made), so the read fails as `Far::Open`, "state.db could not be opened", and nothing of that profile is read. While the gateway runs, both files are there and the read works. A SQLite other than Apple's opens it and leaves a `-wal` and a `-shm` file behind.
- **A read-only open can still make files beside the database.** Where the database's `-shm` file is missing and its `-wal` is there (a log left by a writer that did not close), SQLite makes the `-shm` file to read the log, and the rows that are only in the log are read. That is the spec's "no intentional write", never "nothing written".
- **Not verified here:** Linux; a real SQLite older than 3.43 (the refusal is tested with the system's Python made to say it has one); a far machine other than this Mac behind the lab's `sshd`; a real 3 GB database (ticket 37 measures the Mini). The time of a read in the lab is 0.35 to 0.7 s, nearly all of it Python starting.
## The far control socket (ticket 29)

`src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/far.rs` and `far/relay.py`, with the pure half in `src/crates/agents/src/hermes/gateway/far.rs`. Spec sections 7 and 8 are the design; this is what was built and what a caller must know.

| File | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `agents/.../gateway/far.rs` | `Request` (`Identify`, or `Ticket` with a profile id, an instance id and a scope: the only two there are), `Answered` (an answer, which never prints), `Quiet` and `Unchecked` (why no answer came, each check by name), and `relay_said`, which reads the relay's words. |
| `agents/.../gateway/far/homes.rs` | `HOMES_SCRIPT`, the one discovery command, and `homes`, which reads what it printed into `Homes`: the root, each `FarHome`, a profile and its canonical far path, and the names of the profiles whose home could not be resolved and is not known to be gone (`unresolved`, tickets 57 and 62). The answer is read as bytes. |
| `agents/.../gateway/far/picture.rs` | `picture`: one round of `identify` answers grouped by `instance_id` into `FoundGateway`s, each with the homes whose socket answered as it and the profiles it serves. |
| `platform/.../hermes/far.rs` | `Far`: `exchange` (one request to one home, timed, an empty reply asked once more), `homes`, and `discover`. It remembers which homes have a relay that was cut off. |
| `platform/.../hermes/far/relay.py` | The relay. 300 lines, the standard library only. |
| `platform/.../hermes/far/tests/` | The tests, through ticket 28's lab `sshd` with pretend gateways on real sockets, and `real.rs`, the same against real Hermes gateways when run by hand. |

**What the check on the listener proves, and what it does not.** After it connects, the relay asks the kernel which user the listening process runs as (`getpeereid` through `ctypes` on macOS, `SO_PEERCRED` on Linux) and sends nothing unless it is the login user. That proves the listener runs as the login user, and it catches a listener of any other user. It cannot tell two processes of the same user apart: a second process of that user that takes the socket's place is accepted (`checks::a_second_listener_of_the_same_user_is_accepted_which_is_the_boundary`). Same user on the far machine is the trust boundary, as it is for Hermes's own client and for phase 1 on this Mac. An SSH login as that user is not a stand-in for the check; it is what makes the relay run as that user.

What a caller must know:

- **Only `Request` can be asked.** `Far::exchange` takes one, and the relay itself refuses a line whose verb is not `identify` or `session-ticket`, so a mistake on this side cannot pause a far gateway. A route is handed a line (`Route::exchange`): `Request::of` gives the request that line is, and none for a line that is not one of the two exactly as this side writes them.
- **A home is a canonical far path**, as `Far::homes` gives it. The relay refuses any other spelling (`home-not-canonical`), so one folder is never asked under two names. `Homes::root` is the text `machine_key` needs.
- **The checks are made on open descriptors.** The relay opens the home once, checks that descriptor, and finds the socket from it (a look-up in that folder, then a connection made from inside it). A path that is swapped for another after the check leads nowhere new (`checks::a_path_swapped_after_it_was_opened_does_not_change_what_was_checked`).
- **Where a check cannot be made, the machine is not covered.** No `python3`, no way to ask the kernel who listens, a home someone else may write, an access control list that cannot be read: each is `Unchecked`, by name. Hermes's own client falls back to the path checks where the kernel cannot say; the relay does not.
- **"Closed to others" is the mode bits and the access control list.** On macOS a list can let another user write a folder whose mode is 0700, and Hermes's own client reads only the mode there. The relay reads the list of the home, the socket, the pointer file and the folder it names (`acl_get_fd_np` and `acl_get_link_np` through `ctypes`). A home's list may let someone else read it, as mode 0755 may, and nothing more; the other three may allow no one else anything. An entry that denies opens nothing, and the user's own entries do not count. A list that cannot be read, or holds an entry the relay cannot make out, is `permissions-unread`. On Linux no list is read: a POSIX list's reach is bounded by the group bits, which are checked. On any other system the relay refuses (`lists::`).
- **The relay's deadline is its own.** `Far::new(seconds)` is how long it lives on the far machine, 10 to begin with; a watchdog thread ends it then whatever it is doing, and the kernel's alarm two seconds later if the thread never ran. This side waits two seconds longer than that. `Silent::CutOff` means this side stopped waiting and the relay may still run: until that deadline has surely passed, `exchange` for that home returns `Silent::Busy` and starts nothing. Other homes are not held up.
- **The discovery command has a deadline too** (15 s, a watchdog in the shell), and is held back the same way after a cut-off.
- **An answer is at most 512 KiB**, the size Hermes's own client stops at; a longer one is `Quiet::TooLong`.
- **A ticket is in an `Answered` and nowhere else.** `Answered` prints as `Answered(<masked>)`. No error holds any of the relay's output, and its standard error is never read. Pass the answer to `granted`, which gives a `Ticket` that does not print either.
- **`python3` runs as `python3 -I -S -B -`**: apart from the user's own Python settings and packages, and writing no bytecode. Nothing is written to the far disk by intent, by the relay or by Python for it.
- **A machine with more than 200 profiles is not covered** (`MOST_PROFILES`, `NotCovered::TooManyProfiles`), and neither is one whose homes command prints more than 256 KiB. The homes are asked one after another, so there is a bound, and a machine over it is refused whole: discovery never gives some of the homes as if they were all of them. Folders that lead to one home count once, before anything is counted (`discovery::v_discovery_never_silently_drops_a_valid_home`).
- **A check that fails for any home makes the machine not covered**, with the reason and the home. A home with no socket, or a gateway that is not running, is `Quiet::Absent` in the picture and uncovers nothing. A link lost on the way gives `NoPicture::Unreached`: nothing is known and nothing should change.
- **`Picture::contested`** lists homes that two instances both say they serve, which is a restart overlapping.
- **Not verified here:** Linux (`SO_PEERCRED` and `/proc/self/fd`, the same code, never run), a far Python older than 3.9 (it lacks `fcntl.F_GETPATH` and falls back to resolving the path), a far home on a file system that ignores letter case and is named in another case than its folder (refused as `home-not-canonical`), an NFSv4 access control list on a Linux far machine (only the mode bits are read there), and a far login shell other than zsh.

## The route over SSH (ticket 30)

`src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/route/ssh.rs` and the folder beside it, and `machine.rs`, which follows one far machine over it. Spec sections 1, 7, 9 and 10 are the design; this is what was built and what ticket 32 must know. Nothing in the app starts a far machine yet.

| File | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `machine.rs`, `machine/verify.rs`, `machine/follow.rs` | `FarObserver`: one far machine. It is given a `FarEntry` (a `Target`, the label its cards carry, the control folder, the `ssh` settings), verifies which machine that is, then follows its gateways with ticket 26's `Followers` under the machine's key. `standing()` says how it stands (`Standing`: connecting, connected with the key, not trusted, cannot reach with the reason, not covered with a reason code), and `not_followed()` names the gateways found without the live connection. |
| `route/ssh.rs` | `Ssh`, the second `Route`: homes, exchange, named, prepare, dial and admit. |
| `route/ssh/keeper.rs` | The link's keeper: a thread that opens the link, watches it, and opens it again when it is lost. Each link that opens has a number. |
| `route/ssh/forwards.rs` | One forwarded port for each gateway instance, on one link. |
| `route/ssh/identity.rs` | Which machine the route leads to: the install id every gateway must report, the root home, and which instances have said so on the link that is open. |
| `route/ssh/first.rs` | The first socket to a gateway before anything follows it, which asks for the install's id and is closed. |
| `route/ssh/asking.rs`, `notice.rs` | The far homes, kept for five seconds; what a relay's silence is to the connection; what the route says to the observer (not covered, or a doubt); the gate that keeps commands over one link to four at a time. |

**The order for every socket** is ticket 25's `opened`: `prepare` (the forward is in place), then the ticket through the relay, then the dial through the forward, then `admit`. `links::a_snapshot_and_an_answer_each_take_a_new_ticket_on_the_existing_forward` reads the order back from the route for each thread that opened a socket.

**The forward's port comes from an accepted `Instance` and from nowhere else.** `prepare` is handed the instance the model's `identify()` made, which has checked that its origin is loopback. The route reads the port from that origin and takes it only where the origin is plain `http://127.0.0.1:<port>`: a forward leads to `127.0.0.1` on the far side and nowhere else, so an origin on `127.0.0.2` or `[::1]`, which the model takes for this machine, is refused by the route before any forward or ticket. A gateway whose origin is not loopback at all never gets that far: the model calls it unsupported, and it is reported as not followed.

**One forward for each gateway.** A forward is kept by instance id and used by every socket to that instance. It is cancelled when its instance is followed no more: when the follower of that instance ends (`Route::release`, which a follower calls as it ends, with its model unlocked), when the machine is not followed at all (a verification that did not end in following, and a machine that was uncovered), and when another instance answers at the home it answered at. A cancel that is owed is paid before the next forward is made, so the new instance's forward is never in place beside the one it replaced. The first version matched a replaced forward only by the home it was first asked through, so a gateway that restarted after its follower began asking another of its homes (its anchor profile had left it) kept the old forward open until the link closed; the first review found it (`tests/far/review30.rs`, `review30_restart_after_anchor_retirement_cancels_the_old_forward`). A forward lives on one link; on the next link everything is made again. The table is the newest link's: a socket that comes with the number of an older link is refused as unreachable and changes nothing, where before it emptied the table of the open link, and the forward made there stayed listening with nothing left to cancel it (ticket 50; `route/ssh/forwards/tests_unix.rs`, the third review's probe, on two real links of the lab). Letting a forward go is heard whatever link the caller holds: the forwards keep the link their table is on, and where a release comes holding an older link, or none because it looked while no link was open, what it lets go is cancelled on the table's own link. Before, such a release took the forward out of the table and cancelled nothing, and it listened on until the next forward or release on that link (ticket 51; the same file, the probe of ticket 50's review). That was shown on the forwards alone, called directly on two real links, and not on a running route. By the review's reading of the callers the route needs two unlucky stalls for it: a release comes only from a follower that has already marked itself ended, so a forward prepared for it after that is given up at once and cancelled by the thread that made it, unless that thread stalls too. The table is now right whatever its callers do. A follower whose gateway is stopped and not replaced is still that gateway's follower, and keeps its forward.

**Stopping.** `prepare` hears of a stop, as ticket 25 asks: the forward is made on a thread of its own, the wait for it ends the moment the asker has stopped, and a forward that still arrives is cancelled by the thread that made it (`rules::a_forward_asked_for_by_someone_who_stopped_is_cancelled_and_not_kept`). Stopping the observer ends its followers first, which withdraw their cards and end their readers, then closes the link: the forwards are cancelled, the master is asked to exit and confirmed gone before `stop` returns. A master that is still signing in is ended by its keeper, which gives up the moment it is stopped (`Link::open_while`, new in the link). After a stop no `ssh` of the route runs and nothing answers on its control path (`rules::turning_the_channel_off_withdraws_the_cards_and_leaves_no_master`).

**Identity before anything is told.**

- Before any follower is made, the machine is verified: ticket 29's discovery finds the gateways, and each one that is ready is asked `profiles.list` on a first socket of its own, opened as every socket is. The install ids must all be the same and make a key with the far root home (`machine_key`). The followers are then made with `Machine::Far(key)`, so every session id, request id and reader name of the machine holds the key.
- A missing, empty or malformed id, or two gateways that disagree, make the machine not covered, with the reason (`install_id_missing`, `install_id_empty`, `install_id_length`, `install_id_upper_case`, `install_id_not_hex`, `install_ids_disagree`). No follower exists, so nothing reaches core. It is asked again later, with a growing wait up to half a minute. The connection entry's id is never read by any of this.
- `Route::admit` is the same question on every later first socket: a gateway that turns up after the machine was verified, a gateway that restarted, and every gateway again after the link was lost and opened anew. It is asked before the first poll, on the polls' own socket. An answer that is not the machine's is refused (`install_id_changed`), nothing of that gateway is told, and the machine is uncovered: its followers end, their cards are withdrawn, and it is verified from the start. The same happens where the far root home is another than the key was made from (`root_home_changed`). Who was admitted is kept for the newest link. A socket that took its link a moment before it was replaced, and comes to `admit` with that older number, is held against the machine all the same and then answered yes with nothing changed: it did say who it is, and a refusal here would uncover the machine. Before, it made the route forget who was admitted on the open link (ticket 51; `route/ssh/tests.rs`). The route to this Mac asks nothing: `admit` has a default that says yes.
- **`profiles.list` makes Hermes write an install id where the install has none** (`hermes_cli/install_identity.py`, `read_or_create_install_id`). That is Hermes's own doing, the same as when its desktop app asks, and it happens once for an install. It is the one thing this route can make a far Hermes write without anyone answering a card. The test that reads a real far machine (`tests/far_mini.rs`) looks for the id's file first and stops where there is none.

**A lost link.**

- The followers' sockets run through the forwards, so they end with the master. Each follower takes that as `Input::Lost`, marks its cards (ticket 27) and goes on asking, as it does for a stopped gateway.
- **A lost link is never word that a gateway is gone.** With no link the homes cannot be told (`Silent::Unclear`), and a control socket was not heard (`Silent::TimedOut`). Only the relay's own word, over a link that stands, says that nobody listens (`Silent::Absent`), which is what ticket 26's `gone` goes by. A far home list is never taken for whole (`Inventory::whole` is false): a profile the far listing could not resolve is named apart from the homes (`Homes::unresolved`, tickets 57 and 62), and a `profiles` folder it could not list names nothing. So a follower whose far profile folder was removed stays, asking, and its cards stay marked until the HUD's own hold ends them.
- A command given to a master that is already gone never starts. The route looks at the master's process first (`Link::running`, new in the link, which starts nothing) and hands such a link no command. Without that, ticket 29's rule that a cut-off relay counts as running until its deadline kept a home from being asked for twelve seconds after every lost link.
- The keeper opens the link again after 250 ms, then twice as long each time up to 30 s, and never stops. It looks at the master's process twice a second and asks it every 15 s whether it still answers.

**A machine that fails a check.** The relay refuses a home that others may write, a socket that is not the user's own, and the rest of ticket 29's list. Before a machine is followed, such a home makes it not covered with that reason. While it is followed, a refusal raises a doubt: the observer looks at the whole machine again and uncovers it only where that look says the same, at most every ten seconds, so one home that moved between two readings does not end a machine.

**What the route reports and where.** `Standing` and `not_followed()` are for ticket 32, which fills the machines list from them. The reasons are words for whoever writes the user's text. The log is `hermes-gateway.log`, shared with this Mac's followers through one writer: the machine's own lines begin with `far <destination>`, and its followers' with their gateway id, `<key>/<anchor>`.

**The pace is the connection's own, unchanged.** A far follower runs with `Pace::default()`. Nothing faster was added for far machines.

**What ticket 32 must know.**

- Start one `FarObserver` for each entry that owns a machine, and call `Folder::sweep` once at launch before the first one. The control folder: `~/Library/Caches/com.nickymolina.agenthud/ssh` is 62 bytes on this Mac, which leaves room for a longer user name; the data folder's is 75 of the 77 allowed.
- Dropping the observer is turning it off: cards withdrawn, readers ended, link closed.
- A retargeted entry is a new observer. The key comes from the machine, so the same machine behind a new address has the same session ids.
- One `Far` is kept for the route's whole life, since it holds which homes have a relay that may still run.

**Tests.** `tests/far.rs` and `tests/far/`, through ticket 28's throwaway `sshd` with the pretend gateways of the other tests in a far home of the lab's own. They are marked `#[ignore]` and run by `far::the_lab_tests_pass` in a process of their own. `cards.rs`: a question answered by option and by typed words, an approval with each choice, a request settled on the far side, and a far and a local session of one name as two sessions with two cards. `links.rs`: a gateway restarted mid-question, the master killed mid-question, a machine held out of reach, and what a snapshot and an answer cost. `identity.rs`: the refusals above. `rules.rs`: the rules kept from the tickets before. `review30.rs`: the five probes of the first review, as written. Three more are run by hand: `tests/far_timing.rs`, `tests/far_long.rs` (five minutes out of reach) and `tests/far_mini.rs` (a real far machine, read only).

**The timing suite** (`tests/far_timing.rs`) holds the bounds of spec section 10 and is kept for the tickets that add reads of their own (33, 35, 37, 40), which run it unchanged:

```sh
cargo test -p agent-hud-platform --lib clients::hermes::tests_unix::far_timing -- --ignored --nocapture --test-threads 3
```

The row cases of tickets 34, 54 and 60 (`tests/far_rows_timing.rs`) are run by hand the same way, in the test build and in the optimized build, which is how the app is built:

```sh
cargo test -p agent-hud-platform --lib clients::hermes::tests_unix::far_rows_timing -- --ignored --nocapture --test-threads 2
cargo test --release -p agent-hud-platform --lib clients::hermes::tests_unix::far_rows_timing -- --ignored --nocapture --test-threads 2
```

Both suites are meant to pass with `AGENT_HUD_HISTORY_READS=1` set too, every case of them: the far machine's history (400 stored sessions) is then read back to back while they run (ticket 37). One number is judged in the optimized build only: the 2 s from a label's commit to every row showing it with 5,000 rows in the mirror (ticket 60's `label_on_every_row_with_5000_rows`). Its one pass reads every row, 1.7 to 1.9 s of a test build's time and about 0.4 s of the optimized build's when the case runs alone (1.2 to 1.8 s in the whole suite at a load of 12 to 46), so a test build prints it with `NOT JUDGED` and still judges the case's 6 s (ticket 68).

It runs at the app's pace with the gateway's hints withheld, and each test prints its numbers on a line that begins with `TIMING`. A miss fails the test. It is not part of every test run: it waits on the clock, one test for a minute, and a machine starved by other builds would miss a bound through no fault of the code. Twenty asks are made at twenty moments spread evenly between two polls, the worst one included, so a run does not pass or fail by where its asks happened to fall. A poll's time is the whole poll as the connection times it, with nothing taken off: with one session's events slow to read, the twenty polls are counted from the moment the read turns slow, so the poll that finds the slow read is one of them, and is the one that 19 in 20 leaves room for. The first version took the slow read off each poll's time, which the first review rightly failed.

**Read on a real far machine.** `tests/far_mini.rs` follows a host of the user's own `ssh` configuration and only reads it. It was run against the Mac Mini (Hermes 0.23.1, one gateway for each of two profiles) on 2026-11-14: both gateways ready, each polled only for its own profile, the relay's checks passing on the Mini's Python, 100 polls of each timed (ticket 30's Answer has the numbers). It checks the far disk first, hashes every profile's `config.yaml` before and after, begins no visit (so nothing is subscribed), keeps every command it runs there with how it ended, names every control path a master of its own was started on, and proves on every way out, an early stop included, that `ssh -O check` fails on each of those paths by name and that no `ssh` of its own is left.

**Not verified here:** Linux; a far machine that is neither this Mac nor the Mac Mini; a far login shell other than zsh; answering a card on a real far machine, and a card showing from one (the Mini was only read, with nothing waiting there at the time; ticket 40 is the first to answer there); a session read apart on a real machine (no read on the Mini was slow); a link lost by sleep or a dropped network on a real machine (the master was killed, and the lab's far side was never frozen under a follower); more than two gateways on one machine.

## Machines follow the connection list (ticket 32)

`src/crates/platform/src/clients/hermes/machines.rs` and the folder beside it. Spec "Phase 2 design", sections 1 and 3, are the design; this is what was built and what tickets 33 to 37 must know. `Hooks::configure` starts `hermes::Machines` where it started `hermes::Observer`, so all of it starts and stops with the client hooks setting.

| File | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `machines.rs` | `Machines`: this Mac's `Observer`, the book of far machines, and the thread that looks at the list. `Sources` says where everything is (the app's places, or a lab's). |
| `machines/list.rs` | One look at the file: opened, read, and parsed only where its text is not the last one that parsed. The text is dropped in the function that read it. |
| `machines/apply.rs` | Taking a list that parsed: which entries are new, gone, renamed or readdressed, and what each does. |
| `machines/book.rs`, `keeper.rs` | The book: one slot for each entry (its route, its route number, where its route leads), and the far machines by key (their followers, their owner, the switch their followers dial through). `keeper.rs` is what each route tells it from its own thread. |
| `machines/change.rs` | The steps of a route change, in the spec's order. |
| `route/switch.rs` | `Switch`, the route a far machine's followers hold: the way behind it is put in and taken out, and it has the machine's route number. |
| `handover.rs` | What the followers do at a route change: lose the connection in the step that raises the number, take the new owner's label, dial again. |
| `machine.rs`, `machine/follow.rs` | One entry's route (ticket 30's `FarObserver`, without the followers): the link, the thread that verifies which machine the address leads to, and what it does as the machine's owner or as an alias. `machine/alone.rs` keeps `FarObserver` as a book with one entry, so ticket 30's tests and its timing suite run what the app runs. |
| `machines/bindings.rs` | The bindings file. |
| `machines/status.rs`, `words.rs` | The machines list told to core, and the notes in both languages. |

**The list.** The file's place is ticket 21's `CONNECTIONS` (`~/Library/Application Support/Hermes/connections.json` on macOS). It is looked at every 5 s. A look opens the file, reads it whole (at most 4 MB) and compares its text with the last one that parsed; an equal text is not parsed. A text that changed goes into ticket 21's parser and is dropped. A reading that fails is tried again at every look: only a text that parsed is remembered.

The text is compared by a mark and is not kept (ticket 52). The mark is one 64-bit number made from the text with a key drawn at launch (`Watch::mark`), held in memory and written nowhere, so it says nothing of the text to anyone else. Until ticket 52 a look compared the file's size and time of change, and so never saw a file put back with both as they were (a restore that keeps times, `cp -p`, `rsync -t`). The desktop app does not write that way, and the cost of closing it is one read of a small file every 5 s. A list put back exactly as it last parsed is the same list: it is not parsed again, and the note goes.

- **Only a list that parsed changes anything.** `Book::apply` is called only with a parsed list, and it is the one place an entry is added, removed, renamed or readdressed. A look that failed calls `Book::read(Some(why))`, which sets the note on the whole list and touches nothing else. The note goes when the file is as it last parsed, or parses again.
- **A list equal in every allowlisted field does nothing**, whatever else in the file changed (the desktop app rewrites it when its own token changes): `apply` compares with the list it last took and returns.
- **A list that was never there, with nothing verified before, sets no note.** Hermes's desktop app may not be in use. With bindings, or once a list has parsed, a missing file is a list that could not be read.
- **At launch, until a list parses,** the machines list shows this Mac and each machine verified before, from its binding: its key, its label, "cannot reach", with a note that nothing is dialled. No route exists, so nothing can be dialled.

**Entries, routes, machines.** An entry is a row of the list. A route is one connection for an entry (`machine.rs`), and the entry's route number counts the addresses it has had. A machine is a key, and its followers (`Followers`, ticket 26) are made once, when a route first verifies as it, and belong to it until it is retired.

- **One owner.** The first entry in the list's order whose route verified as the machine. Which entry verifies first at launch is a race, so where a later entry's route got there first, the earlier entry takes the machine from it when it verifies: one route change in the first seconds, which registers nothing again. A route that verifies as a machine that has an owner is an alias (`Standing::Alias`): it follows nothing, lets go of what verifying made, keeps its link, and waits to be asked to verify again. An alias whose entry comes before the owner's in the list takes the machine as soon as it verifies. A route that verifies as this Mac is an alias too. This Mac's key is made, the way a far one is, from the id in its Hermes root's `install_id` file and that root with its links resolved (`Local::key`); the file is read, never written.
- **A route change** is `change.rs`, and it follows the spec's five steps. `cut` raises the switch's number and, in the same locked step, has every follower of the machine take a lost socket (`Followers::lose`): the cards are marked in place with `mark_native`, an answer being written is cut, a decision core took and has not handed over is stale, and a poll under way is dropped by the model when it comes back. From that step the switch has no way, so nothing more is asked of the old route, and what the old route still hands back (a stream it dialled, a ticket) is refused by the switch because the number is another. Only after the book's lock is let go is the old route stopped, which closes its link.
- **The same machine goes on.** `install` puts the new owner's route behind the switch, gives the machine that entry's label, and stirs the followers, whose first poll finds what still waits and takes its mark away. Core's `register_native` and `settle_native` are not called: a card keeps its request, the time it came, its hold and whether it was dismissed.
- **Another machine, or an identity that is refused.** The machine waits on every entry that may yet lead to it (`Far::pending`): the readdressed entry's new route, and each alias, which is asked to verify anew. An alias whose address was edited is still the machine's alias until its new route verifies or is reached and refused (ticket 52): the new slot keeps `Lead::Alias`. So where the owner is removed and the alias readdressed in one look, or the alias was readdressed earlier and has not been reached since, the machine waits on the alias's new route and is not retired and made again. That is the rule a readdressed owner already had, and it makes the outcome the same whichever of the two edits comes first. The cost: where the alias's new address cannot be reached, the old machine waits instead of being withdrawn, so its marked cards can sit beside a new machine's until their 10-minute hold runs out. A waiting card refuses answers, and the old machine is withdrawn at once when the alias is removed, leads elsewhere, or is refused. An entry leaves that set when its route verifies as another machine, when it is reached and refused (any "not covered" reason but a stopped gateway or a missing live connection, which say nothing of which machine it is), when it is edited into an entry that is never dialled, and when it leaves the list. A route that cannot be reached stays in it, and so does an entry whose address is edited again while the machine waits on it: the machine waits on the entry's newest route. A machine that waits has no way behind its switch, so its route number stays as the one change left it. With the set empty and no owner, the machine is retired: `Followers::stop`, which withdraws its cards and ends its readers. That happens in the step in which the entry's route reported the other machine, before that machine's followers are made.
- **The owner's route is told that it leads there no more** (`Noticed::Uncovered`: a gateway reports another install, the root home moved, a check fails) exactly as in ticket 30. It is now a route change: an alias takes the machine over, and only with none is it retired.

**The route number and answers.** The adapter's rule from spec section 9 ("the route number is the one the card was last reconciled under") is held by the switch and the mark together. A card is unmarked only by a poll that counted, and a poll counts only if it began after the last lost socket, which the raise is. Every socket, a visit's included, is opened through the switch, which refuses a stream dialled under another number. So an answer goes out only on a route whose own poll found the request waiting.

**The bindings.** `hermes-machines.json` in the data folder, written whole and for this user alone: `{version: 1, bindings: [{entry, host, user?, port?, keyPath?, key, label}]}`. An entry is written when its route verifies as a far machine (an alias too), relabelled when the entry is, and dropped when the entry leaves a list that parsed. A key read back is a `StoredKey` (ticket 23), from which no route, poll, card or answer can be made. The bindings give an entry's status its machine's key while it is not connected, where the entry still has the address it verified at and is the one entry that goes by that key (see the machines list).

**The machines list.** One status for each entry, in the list's order, and this Mac first where the list has no `local` entry.

- `id` is the machine's key once the entry verified, now or before (the 16 hex characters its sessions' ids begin with), and the entry's id until then. An alias goes by its entry's id.
- **No two statuses share an id** (ticket 52). One status goes by a machine's key (`State::named`): its owner's, and while it has no owner (nothing is followed yet, or it waits for one), that of the first entry in the list's order that verified as it at the address it has now. Every other entry bound to the machine goes by its entry's id, whether it stands as "same machine", "cannot reach", "connecting" or anything else. An entry's id that could be taken for another status's goes by `entry:` and that id (ticket 53): one shaped like a machine key, the word `local` (this Mac's own status where the list has no entry for it), and one that already begins with `entry:`. So which entry carries the key can change at a relaunch with the machine offline: from a later entry that owned it to the first one bound to it. The key itself never changes. Settings keys its rows by `id`.
- `here` is set on this Mac's own entry alone. An `ssh` entry that reaches this Mac is "same machine", with this Mac's label as its note.
- States: connected (the owner, link up), connecting, not trusted yet, cannot reach (with the link's reason), not covered (with the reason), same machine (with the other entry's label as the note, as ticket 38 asked).
- `lastContactAt` and `historyCheckedAt` are not set: they come from the mirror (tickets 34 and 37).
- The list is told again at every look, since a link comes and goes without a word to the book. So a link that drops shows within 5 s.
- With client hooks off, no list is told.

**Not trusted yet.** A host whose key is unknown or changed is tried again every five minutes (`route/ssh/keeper.rs`, `after`), where any other failure is tried again within half a minute. Only the user can change it, in a file that is not this app's. `known_hosts` is never written (ticket 28).

**The control folder** is `~/Library/Caches/com.nickymolina.agenthud/ssh`. `Folder::sweep` runs once, on the thread that looks at the list, before its first look and so before the first route.

**What tickets 33 to 37 must know.**

- A machine's followers are `Book`'s, by key. A writer that belongs to the machine (ticket 34's mirror writer) should be made where the followers are (`Book::claim`) and ended where they are (`retire`).
- `Switch::number()` is the machine's route number. A write that carries the number it was read under can be held against it.
- The label a machine goes by is `Far::label`, which `relabel` and `install` change.
- `FarObserver` is still there, as a book with one entry given directly and no list. The timing suite uses it unchanged.

**Tests.** `tests/machines.rs` is the lab: a list file of the test's own, one throwaway `sshd` for each far host, the pretend gateways, and a real core, so what a test reads of the machines list, the cards and the alerts is what a snapshot says. The tests are in `tests/machines/` (`lists.rs`, `owners.rs`, `answers.rs`, `reasons.rs`), marked `#[ignore]` and run by `machines::the_lab_tests_pass` in a process of their own. The switch, the list's reader, the bindings and the retry wait have unit tests beside them.

**Not verified here:** a real far machine (nothing in this ticket contacts the Mac Mini); the real `connections.json`, which no test and no worker opened; Linux; two entries that reach one real machine by two networks.

## The mirror, and far sessions as rows (ticket 34)

Spec "Phase 2 design", section 12, is the design; this is what was built and what tickets 33, 35 and 37 must know.

| File | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `agents/src/hermes/mirror.rs` | What a mirror is to the reader (ticket 23), and `ROOT`, the mirrors' folder under the data folder. |
| `agents/src/hermes/mirror/write.rs` | The write side, pure: `file` and `files` (where a mirror is), `claim` (the tables and the one `hud_mirror` row), `relabel`, `list` (a row for each listed session the mirror lacks). Each takes the connection it writes on. |
| `agents/src/hermes/gateway/listing.rs` | `Gateway::listing()`: what the newest poll that counted said of each live session (`Listed`: title, model, source, start), profile by profile, and when that poll came back. None while the gateway is not ready or has not been polled since it last was. |
| `platform/src/clients/hermes/mirror.rs`, `mirror/files.rs` | `Writer`: the queue, the thread, and one transaction on one mirror. `Routes` is what it asks of the machine's route switch. |
| `platform/src/hooks/changed.rs` | `Changed`, the callback a native channel is given, and the paths that wait for core. |

**Where, and how core finds it.** `<data folder>/clients/hermes-far/profiles/<key>-<profile>/state.db`. The Hermes adapter's sources are now per system: its home, and on macOS and Linux the mirrors' folder in Agent HUD's data folder (`AGENTHUD_DATA_DIR` names that folder in tests, as for Cursor's turn log). The folder is a root whether or not it exists yet. The shared walker lists a Hermes database only in a folder that holds `state.db` or a `profiles` folder, is named `profiles`, or sits directly under one; this layout passes that rule unchanged. The path carries no meaning: the reader takes the machine, the label and the profile from `hud_mirror`.

**The tables** are spec section 12's, as written there: `hud_mirror` (`schema` 1, `machine`, `label`, `profile`), `hud_rows` (`session_id`, `durable`, `read_ms`, `print`, `held`), `hud_live` (`session_id`, the four token counts, `read_ms`) and `hud_reads` (`contact_ms`, `sweep_cursor`, `sweep_started_ms`, `sweep_done_ms`), beside Hermes's `sessions` and `session_model_usage` with the columns the reader selects (`far_history::SESSION_COLUMNS`, `USAGE_COLUMNS`) and nothing required that a live list may not say. This ticket writes `hud_mirror`, `sessions` and `hud_rows`, and makes `hud_reads` with its one empty row; `hud_live` is ticket 35's and the two ages in `hud_reads` are ticket 37's. A mirror is in write-ahead mode, so core reads it while it is written. Its folders are 0700 and the file 0600.

**One writer.** `Book::claim` makes the writer with the machine, before its followers, and `Followers::stop` ends it, which is what retiring the machine and turning client hooks off both call. It belongs to the machine's key: a handover or a new address leaves the same writer on the same file.

- Every write goes on one queue, and one thread takes them in order. A follower queues what its poll listed after it let go of its model; the thread holds nobody's lock while it writes, but for the switch's in the moment it commits.
- The queue holds 64 writes at most. A disk that stalls drops the oldest: every poll lists its sessions anew.
- A poll that lists nothing new opens nothing and writes nothing. The writer remembers which sessions each mirror holds, and looks whether the file is still there before it trusts that.
- `stop` returns once no write is under way and every mirror is closed. Closing folds the write-ahead log into the file, and core is told of that too, so the file core listed is the file as it stays.

**What a write carries.**

- *When its data was read* (`Listing::read_ms`, when the poll came back). A listing of a profile read no later than the newest one taken for that profile is dropped. That also drops a listing a follower hands over twice.
- *The route number it was read under.* The link thread reads the switch's number before it asks who is at the home, and again once the socket is open: a socket opened across a route change is closed and opened again, so every poll's socket was opened under one number, and that number goes with what the poll listed (`Shared::under`). The writer drops a write whose number is not the machine's now when it takes it off the queue, and commits inside `Routes::under`, which holds the switch while it compares the number and commits. So raising the number waits for a commit that is under way (a write to a local file) and for nothing else, and no write read under an old number is committed after the number went up.
- A retarget to another install retires the old machine, whose writer has stopped; the new machine has a writer and a switch of its own, counting from 1. A late poll of the old machine can reach only the old writer.

**Rows from the live list.** `session.active_list` already returns each live session's `title`, `model`, `source` and `started_at`; the poll kept only the id and the status, and now keeps those too (`Look::listed`). Nothing more is asked of the gateway. `mirror::list` inserts a `sessions` row and a `hud_rows` row (`durable` 0, the read time, no fingerprint) for a session that has neither, and touches nothing else, so a durable row and a row made before are both left exactly as they are. A session whose list gives no start is taken to have started when it was read, since the reader skips a session without one. Titles are cut at 512 characters and the other words at 128.

**The label.** The writer has the machine's label, which `Followers::relabel` gives it (a renamed entry, or a handover to an entry of another label). A change queues one write that sets `hud_mirror.label` in every mirror of the machine there is, whichever profile, and the writer does the same when it starts, so an entry renamed while its machine was not followed reaches the rows when it is next verified. The label is part of each session's fingerprint (ticket 23), so the next pass reads every session of the machine again and nothing is counted twice.

**Telling core.** `hooks::Changed` is `Fn(&[PathBuf])`: it keeps the paths and calls the channel's wake. It names no client and reads nothing. The wake asks the collector for a request turn, which has no spacing. `Runtime::request` (`note_written`) hands the paths to `AgentHudCore::file_changes`, the entry the file watcher uses, and where there were any it runs that turn as a pass (`snapshot`) in place of `request_snapshot`. The shared walker and the stat check between passes are not edited.

- Why not the file watcher's own wake: the collector reads file events at most every 2 s. A write just after a pass would wait out those 2 s, and "within 2 s of its commit" could not hold at every moment.
- Why it is needed at all: the stat check between passes looks at 64 logs core has read before, a folder made after launch is watched only from the next pass on, and every root is walked again only each 300 s. The file watcher reports the same writes when it can, and that only makes the read sooner.

**Timing** (spec section 10), in the lab at the app's own pace, with the gateway's hints withheld and no file watcher at all. Each time is from the turn starting on the far machine to core listing the row.

| Case | Bound | Measured, 20 runs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `row_when_the_gateway_was_busy` | 6 s | 86 to 953 ms, middle 574 ms, none over (machine load about 300 from other builds) |
| `row_when_it_was_idle` (nothing was listed) | 15 s | 0.46 to 10.0 s, middle 5.6 s, none over (the same load). The starts were spread evenly over the ten seconds between two polls, so 10 s is the pace, not chance |

From a commit to the row being listed, in the writer's own tests: 82 ms for a first mirror, 12 ms for a write that changed only the write-ahead log, 527 ms for a mirror outside the 64 newest logs (with 70 other logs just indexed), each held to 2 s.

**What tickets 33, 35 and 37 must know.**

- The writer of a machine is `Followers::mirror`. A new kind of write is a new `Write` on its queue: give it the time its data was read and the route number it was read under, and commit through `Files::write` with that number.
- `mirror::list` never touches a row that is there. Ticket 37 replaces a live row with a durable one itself, in its own transaction.
- A zero-usage row writes no ledger event. Its key is `usage:<id>:[<model>,"","","",""]`, so a durable row of the same model with no billing fields would add to that key from zero, which is right.
- **The reader pages 100 sessions a pass.** (Changed by ticket 54: see "Grown mirrors, and reads that failed" below.) Core reads one page of a database in each pass and goes on from the cursor at the next, 2 s later while reads wait. A mirror with thousands of stored rows therefore takes one pass for each 100 rows before a new row at its end is listed, and each change to the file starts that over from the watermark. Today's mirrors hold live rows only, so this does not show. Ticket 37 must measure the row bounds on a mirror the size of the Mini's history, and may need the reader's order or page changed.
- A read-only reader leaves an empty `state.db-wal` beside a mirror that was closed. That is SQLite's doing and changes no row.

**Not built here:** token counts (ticket 35), stored history and the two ages of the machines list (ticket 37), and the turn facts for alerts (ticket 33). An entry renamed while its machine is not followed keeps the old label on its rows until the machine is next verified, since only a followed machine has a writer.

**Tests.** `agents/src/hermes/mirror/tests.rs` (the write side), `gateway/tests/listing.rs` (the model), `transcript_tests.rs` (ticket 23's reader tests, now also on a mirror `claim` made), `platform/src/clients/hermes/mirror/tests.rs` (the writer with a real core and no file watcher), `tests/machines/mirrors.rs` (the lab: the machines the app runs, a far gateway behind a throwaway `sshd`, a real core), `tests/far_rows_timing.rs` (the two bounds, by hand), `core/src/tests/hermes_mirror.rs` (the ledger), `runtime/src/written_tests.rs` (the runtime's part).

**Not verified here:** a real far machine (nothing in this ticket contacts the Mac Mini); Linux; a mirror with stored history in it.

## Grown mirrors, and reads that failed (ticket 54)

Ticket 34's verifier found two states in which a far session's row did not show in time (`evidence/34/verifier-report.md`, findings 1 and 2), and three smaller faults (findings 3 to 5). This is what was changed, why this way, and what tickets 35 and 37 must know.

### A new row, whatever the mirror holds

**What was wrong.** `hermes::transcript::read` read a hundred sessions a page from the lowest rowid up, and core reads one page of a database in a pass. A change to the file began the pass anew. So a session that began, whose row is the last, was read at the end: 1 pass at 90 rows, 3 at 250, 11 at 1,000, with the passes two seconds apart. Rows made from the live list are never removed, so every mirror grows into this, and ticket 37 adds whole histories.

**The choice.** The newest rows are read first. The ticket named three ways; the other two were not taken:

- *Removing ended live rows* would keep a mirror of live rows small, and breaks the design twice. Spec section 12 keeps a mirror as the machine's history ("stays as history", and section 9 shows a machine's stored sessions while it cannot be reached), so a session would leave the list when it ended and come back when ticket 37 read it from the far database. And ticket 37 fills the mirrors with thousands of durable rows anyway, which nothing may remove.
- *Going on where the last pass stopped, without beginning anew*, cannot be had: Hermes adds to a session in place, with no change a cursor could go on from, so a pass has to look at the old rows again to find one that grew (`session_counts`). What can be had is that the new rows do not wait for that, which is the choice.
- *A larger page* for every database only moves the row count at which it fails, and a page of Hermes's own database has no bound on what a row costs to read. It is used for mirrors alone, below, where it removes work.

**How a page reads now** (`agents/src/hermes/transcript.rs`, with the rows in `transcript/rows.rs` and `transcript/usage.rs`):

1. *New sessions first.* Those above the highest rowid read so far (`max_rowid` in the cursor), the newest hundred, in the table's order. SQLite gives a new row a rowid above every one there is, so this is exact for rows that are added.
2. *Then what an earlier page had to pass over.* Where more than a hundred began, the rest are a stretch of rowids kept in the cursor (`gaps`); a later page with room reads from its newest end. Each new session is read once.
3. *Then the pass over the old ones*, those up to the rowid the last pass ended with (`rowid_after`), from the lowest up, reading the ones whose counts grew or that were active since, as before. A hundred a page.

A pass ends when the old rows are through and no stretch waits. The first read of a database takes its newest sessions first too. A cursor the reader saved before this change is read as it is, mid-pass or not; the reader's version is not raised and nothing is read again for the change.

**A mirror's pass over its old rows ends in one page.** With the new rows read first, the row showed at once, and core still made 50 more passes for a mirror of 5,000 rows after every write, two seconds apart, each costing what a pass costs with 5,000 sessions listed (about a quarter of a second of CPU in a test build, most of it the snapshot). On a busy machine those passes run into each other, and the pass a new row waits for queues behind them. So in a mirror, and only in a mirror, a page looks at up to 10,000 old rows (`MIRROR_PAGE`) and still reads a hundred at most. A mirror's rows are short, since its writer cuts every word it stores, and nothing but the writer changes them. The same rows are looked at as before, once for each write, in the pass that write asked for. Hermes's own `state.db` keeps the hundred: a row of it can hold anything.

**Measured** (the writer's tests with a real core, `mirror/tests/large.rs`; half the rows as the live list leaves them, half as the far database gives them, with tokens and a usage row):

| Rows in the mirror | Before: passes until the new row is listed | Now | Now: passes after it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 90 | 1 | 1 | 0 |
| 250 | 3 | 1 | 0 |
| 1,000 | 11 | 1 | 0 |
| 5,000 | 51 | 1 | 0 |

**Timing** (spec section 10), in the lab as for ticket 34: the app's own pace, the gateway's hints withheld, no file watcher. With rows in the mirror already, the collector's turns are modelled on the app's (`host/src/service/collector.rs`): one at once when a writer asks, and one every two seconds while core says logs wait. Twenty runs of each, in a test build, on the rebased tree, the machine's load between 4 and 11 (`evidence/54/timing/run-3-debug.txt`):

| Case | Bound | Rows in the mirror | Least, middle, most | Over |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A row when the gateway was busy | 6 s | 1 to 21 | 41, 533, 828 ms | 0 |
| | | 1,000 | 73, 589, 904 ms | 0 |
| | | 5,000 | 256, 775, 1,292 ms | 0 |
| A row when it was idle | 15 s | 1 to 21 | 0.44, 5.4, 9.9 s | 0 |
| | | 1,000 | 0.51, 5.5, 10.0 s | 0 |
| | | 5,000 | 0.72, 5.8, 10.2 s | 0 |
| From the row's commit to its listing | 2 s | 1,000 (busy runs) | 48, 69, 85 ms | 0 |
| | | 1,000 (idle runs) | 46, 75, 98 ms | 0 |
| | | 5,000 (busy runs) | 224, 253, 578 ms | 0 |
| | | 5,000 (idle runs) | 201, 251, 932 ms | 0 |

In an optimized build, which is how the app is built, the same cases at a load of 5 to 14 (`timing/run-4-release.txt`): every row within its bound, and from commit to listing 11 to 51 ms at 1,000 rows and 44 to 905 ms at 5,000 (middle 56 and 86 ms), none over. No optimized run was made under heavy load.

The idle times are the pace of the polls, ten seconds apart, as in ticket 34. Against ticket 34's code the same cases show no row within 12 s at 1,000 rows or at 5,000 (`evidence/54/red/all.txt`).

**Under heavy load the 2 s does not hold in a test build, and the row bounds do.** An earlier run of the same cases was made while another worker's builds held the machine at a load of 209 to 375, on 12 cores (`timing/run-2.txt`; the tree before the rebase, and with the test's own look at the list still inside the measured time). Every row was within 6 s and 15 s, at both sizes. From commit to listing, 1,000 rows held at load 209 (most 884 ms) and missed at load 375 (most 2.4 s, 6 of 20 over); 5,000 rows missed at loads 226 and 243 (most 2.7 s and 5.4 s, 8 and 16 of 20 over). The reason is under "What a pass costs" below: a pass with 5,000 sessions listed costs about 0.3 s of a test build's CPU time, so at twenty times the machine's cores one pass alone is longer than 2 s.

**What still takes a pass for every hundred rows.** A page reads a hundred sessions at most, so a change to every row of a mirror at once is read over as many passes as before: a new label reaches 1,000 rows in 10 passes and 5,000 in 50, two seconds apart. A new row is still listed at the first pass while that goes on (test `a_new_row_is_listed_at_the_first_pass_while_the_mirror_is_being_read`). A mirror of more than 10,000 rows takes one more pass for each 10,000.

**What a pass costs is core's, and it grows with the sessions listed.** With 5,000 sessions a pass that reads one row costs about 0.3 s of CPU in a test build, two thirds of it the snapshot (`render_snapshot`: the usage projection, the session rows, the meters) and a sixth the read. That is the same for 5,000 sessions of any client. It is why the 2 s from commit to listed cannot hold on a machine whose load is many times its cores: one pass alone is longer. The timing table says at what load each run was made.

### A read that failed

**What was wrong.** Core does not read a log again for 30 s, doubling to 300 s, after a read of it failed, and a file told through `file_changes` was left be all the same. A mirror had no tables from the moment its file was made until its first commit, and for good where that first write was dropped at the route check or refused. Read then, it paused; the verifier measured 30.1 s from the commit that made it readable to its row.

**Two changes, either of which closes it.**

- *The writer's word ends the pause.* `AgentHudCore::written_files(paths)` is `file_changes` for files a native channel says it wrote itself: the paths are changed files, and a pause that runs for one of them ends, so the pass that follows reads it. A read that fails again pauses for longer, as after any failure in a row. `Runtime::note_written` hands told files there. A file event does not end a pause: the watcher reports half a write as readily as a whole one, and the pause is there for that.
- *There is no mirror without its tables.* `mirror/files.rs`, `make`: a mirror that is not there is made as `state.db.new` in its own folder, which core reads no file by the name of; the first transaction is committed into it under the route check as every write is; it is closed, which folds the write-ahead log in; and then it is renamed to `state.db`. A first write that is not committed, for whatever reason, leaves no file under either name, and one left by a writer that was cut off is discarded before the next is made. The mirror is opened again and stays open, as one that was there already.

### An entry with no label

The list's reader takes an `ssh` entry with no `label`. Its machine was followed and had cards, and `mirror::claim` refuses an empty label, as the reader of ticket 23 does, so it had no rows, a log line at every poll and an empty file. The reader now gives such an entry its host for a label (`connections.rs`), the host of the checked address and not the text as typed. Other kinds are not followed and keep an empty one. What the desktop app itself shows for such an entry is not known: the real list was not opened. The writer still refuses an empty label, logs a failure once for as long as every write fails the same way (`Files::fails`), and leaves no file.

### A label that must not be lost

The write queue held 64 writes and dropped the oldest, which could be the one write of a new label, never queued again. It now holds 64 listings and drops the oldest of those; a label write is never dropped, and a second change while one waits queues nothing, since the write takes the label as it is when its turn comes.

### The route number a listing carries

No test failed when a poll's listing was stamped with the route's number as it was when queued, in place of the number its socket was opened under. `tests/route_number.rs`, `a_poll_is_written_under_the_route_number_its_socket_was_opened_under`, raises a route's number and tells the follower nothing, so the listing's number is all that keeps it out of the mirror.

### For tickets 35 and 37

> Ticket 60 changed the second point: a mirror's page reads every changed row it looks at, up to 10,000, so a change to every row at once (a new label) is read in one pass.

- **Never delete a mirror's newest row.** A row is known for new by its rowid being above every one read. SQLite gives a row put in after the highest one was deleted that same rowid again, and it is then found by the pass over the old rows like any other, a page later at most in a mirror. Replace a live row by `UPDATE`, in place.
- **A change to a row that is there** (a live count, a durable row over a live one, `held`) is found by the pass over the old rows, which a mirror now makes in the page the write asked for: one pass up to 10,000 rows. It reads a hundred changed sessions a page, so a batch that changes more than a hundred rows at once is read a hundred a pass.
- **A first sync** of thousands of new rows is read a hundred a pass, newest first. A session that begins meanwhile is read at the next pass.
- **Every commit is a pass**, with no spacing, and a pass costs what core's snapshot costs with every session listed. The verifier's warning stands: commit real changes only.
- **Tell core through the writer's callback.** A file told that way is read though its last read failed.

**Tests.** `agents/src/hermes/transcript_tests.rs` (the page: new first, each once, a pass under way, a mirror's page), `connections/tests.rs`, `core/src/tests/hermes_mirror.rs` (the pause), `runtime/src/written_tests.rs`, `platform/src/clients/hermes/mirror/tests/large.rs` and `tests/failed.rs` (the writer with a real core), `tests/route_number.rs`, `tests/machines/mirrors.rs` (the lab: an entry with no label), `tests/far_rows_timing.rs` (the bounds with 1,000 and 5,000 rows, by hand).

**Not verified here:** a real far machine; the app's own collector, which the timing cases model from its source; Linux and Windows.

## Sounds for a far machine (ticket 33)

Spec "Phase 2 design", section 11, is the contract; this is what was built.

| File | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `agents/src/hermes/gateway/turns.rs` | `Turn` (session, the stream's epoch, Hermes's count of the turn, started or ended) and its form as a hook event; the places the model keeps between polls; `Gateway::take_turns`. |
| `agents/src/hermes/gateway/turns/watch.rs` | What one poll learns of the streams: the looks, the reads that follow a look, and what the card reads say of the turns. |
| `platform/src/clients/hermes/turns.rs` | Telling core each turn, and the rest a session gets when a look at it was slow. |
| `platform/src/hooks/told.rs` | `Told`, the callback a native channel is given to say what a hook would have reported, and the queue that waits for the collector. |
| `core/src/sound_alerts/kept.rs` | The alert tracker's decided identities, saved and loaded. |

**What is promised, in plain words.**

- A far question or approval sounds `needs-you` once. It does not sound again after the HUD is relaunched, however late its card comes back. One asked while the HUD was closed sounds when its card arrives.
- A far turn that ends sounds `finished` once, if the HUD was connected to that machine when it ended and had already seen the session.
- No `finished` for a turn that ended while the machine was out of reach, or while the HUD was closed.
- No `finished` for a session that opens, runs and closes between two polls, or that leaves the live list before its end was read.
- No `finished` for whatever is already in a session's stream when the HUD first sees it, also after a reconnect or a relaunch.
- No `finished` where Hermes has dropped the completion event before it was read. Hermes keeps the last 512 events and 4 MiB of a stream.
- A step or a tool call makes no sound. A handover from one entry to another makes none.
- A turn its user stopped sounds `finished` like any other end. That is what this Mac's Hermes hook does too (`on_session_end` fires for both).
- This Mac's sessions are as they were: their hooks tell their turns, and the followers of this Mac's gateways add nothing.

**When it was seen, not when it ended.** A far completion's time, on the hook event, in the row's `liveFacts.endedAt` and on the island's event (`at`), is the time the poll that read the event came back. Hermes's `message.complete` carries no time of its own (`tests/fixtures/hermes/gateway-turns/a-turn-read-after-a-look.json` is one real answer, with every field it has). A look can fail or rest for a long while and the end be read much later, so nothing in the code, the tests or these notes says how long before it was seen a turn ended. A far turn's start is told with no start time at all.

**The reads.** Only a far machine's gateways take them (`Turns::of`: the gateway's machine is a far one).

- *A look.* `Step::Watch` with no cursor is `session.events.since` with no epoch, which Hermes answers with the stream's epoch and latest sequence and no event. Every listed session the poll does not read anyway gets one in each poll: one call for each idle session.
- *A read that follows.* Where the stream is in the epoch it was seen in and has moved past its place, the same poll reads on from the place. Where Hermes refuses that, having dropped the events after the place, the poll searches for the oldest event Hermes still keeps, as a card read does, and never below the place: a completion that is still kept is found by its sequence.
- *What the polls read anyway.* A session that works, waits or shows a card is read whole at every poll for its cards. Its turns are taken from those same answers, at no call more.
- A session read apart from the polls (ticket 30) has its turns told from its own readings.
- The looks go out after the card reads of a round, on the same socket. They are never handed to a lane and never keep one going.

**The places.** One for each listed session: the epoch, and the latest sequence a read of this connection reached. A place is forgotten when the socket is lost, when the gateway restarts, and when its session leaves the list. A poll that is dropped (not the newest, or come back after the socket was lost) sets none and tells nothing. Facts are told against the place the model holds when the poll comes back, so a poll and a reading apart of the same session never tell one event twice.

**A turn's name.** `<epoch>:<generation>`. Hermes counts a session's turns within one run of a gateway, and core keeps each `(source, session, turn)` it was told ended for 30 days, so the count alone would silence a later turn of the same number.

**Telling core.** After everything the model takes in, the follower takes the turns told (`Shared::turned`) and gives each to `hooks::Told` as a hook event of the source: `session-observation` with state `running` for a start, `completion` for an end, with the fields this Mac's hook gives and the machine's label beside them. `Told` keeps them in order and calls the channel's wake; the collector's turn hands them to core with what the hooks reported (`Hooks::drain_events`). They are kept in memory only. Two ends of one session told in one poll are two events: the hook inbox, which keeps the newest record of a kind for a session, is not used.

**A look that is slow.** A look, or the read after one, that takes as long as a slow card read (`Pace::apart`, 250 ms) or is never answered, rests its session: no look at it for six times `Pace::answer` (30 s). The read after a look is never held back. The place is kept, so what the session's turns did meanwhile is told when it is looked at again.

**Alert decisions across a relaunch.** `sound-alerts.json` in the data folder holds each decided identity with when it was last among the tracker's inputs: `{"version":1,"decided":{"<identity>":<ms>}}`. It is written at once when an identity is decided or forgotten, and at most every ten minutes otherwise, since the times move at every look. At load, identities older than a day are dropped, and a time from a clock that ran ahead is taken as now. It names sessions and requests and holds no text anyone wrote. A file that cannot be read is an empty one.

**Timing** (spec section 10): ticket 30's suite, unchanged, in the lab at the app's own pace with the gateway's hints withheld. Every follower in it is a far one, so the looks are on throughout. One case was added, with ten sessions listed idle beside a busy one. Times are from Hermes asking to the card being in core, in ms.

| Case | Bound | Run 2, machine load about 10 (least, middle, most) | Run 1, load about 240 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `card_in_a_busy_session` | 19 of 20 within 4000, none over 6000 | 3, 476, 792 | 33, 537, 822 |
| `card_beside_a_showing_card` | the same | 25, 527, 825 | 16, 520, 830 |
| `card_with_five_busy_sessions` | the same | 26, 513, 824 | 13, 485, 821 |
| `card_behind_a_slow_replay` | the same | 22, 518, 819 | 8, 509, 825 |
| `card_in_the_session_that_is_slow_to_read` | the same | 89, 1275, 1943 | 206, 1166, **8370: a miss** |
| `card_with_ten_idle_sessions` (new) | the same | 3, 473, 794, with 620 looks in 62 polls | 34, 531, 823, with 630 looks in 63 polls |
| `card_in_an_idle_listed_session` | 13000 | 729 | 5719 |
| `card_when_nothing_was_listed` | 13000 | 3916, 6928, 9947 | 3940, 6938, 9906 |
| `a_request_no_read_shows` | 32000 | 28449 | 28822 |
| `poll_time_over_the_link` | 19 of 20 within 1000 | 1, 1, 6 | 1, 3, 19 |
| the same with a slow replay | the same | one poll over, at 2013 | one poll over, at 2010 |
