### Leftovers after the verifier (ticket 61)

Ticket 37's verifier passed it with seven small findings (`evidence/37/verifier-report.md`). This is what was done with them.

**The pace goes by work (the coordinator's decision on finding 2).** "Live" meant "listed". The Mini's default gateway lists about 25 sessions all the time, nearly all idle, so the refresh never left its quick pace: 22 to 24 far reads a minute, each a `python3` and a `ps` on the Mini, about 34,000 a day, for as long as the app was open. Now:

- `Gateway::working` (agents) says whether a session of the newest poll that counted is in a turn: `starting` or `working`. Idle is not, however many are listed. Waiting for an answer is not either: its counts stand still until the answer is given, and a question may wait for hours.
- Each poll that finds one tells the machine's mirror writer when (`Writer::worked`), and `Writer::working` says whether that was within the last two minutes (`plan::FRESH`, the bound the live list already had). So the tick after a turn's end still comes at the quick pace, and reads the counts the turn ended with.
- `Plan::wait` picks the pace after each tick: 60 s while a session works, a whole sweep is overdue, or the tick could read nothing; 5 minutes otherwise; always 5 minutes for a machine with no history reader.
- A wait longer than the quick pace looks once in that pace whether a session has taken a turn, and ends if one has (`History::pause`). The look is a lock on this Mac and nothing else. Without it a turn that began just after a tick would wait out the five minutes first.
- The first queue is unchanged: every listed session is still read by id at each tick, idle ones too, which on the Mini is one read. Only how often a tick comes changed. No timing value changed: 10 s, 60 s, 300 s, 24 hours and the two minutes are as they were.

In the lab (`many_idle_listed_sessions_leave_the_slow_pace_and_a_working_one_brings_the_quick_one`), by the clock, with a quick pace of 200 ms and a slow one of ten minutes: 25 idle listed sessions and a finished first sync, no tick in 3 s; one of the 25 takes a turn, five ticks in about 4 s. Against ticket 37's code the same test counts two ticks in the 3 s.

**A session the mirror refuses (finding 6).** Hermes's usage table is keyed by session, model, provider, base URL, mode and task. The far reader sends a value that is a blob, or not valid text, as none, and the mirror stores none in a key column as the empty text. So two far rows that differ only in such a value come to one name in the mirror, whose primary key refuses the second; a Hermes version that adds a column to that key would do the same. The whole batch of up to 25 sessions was rolled back and asked for again at every tick, and the tick stopped for the machine's other profiles.

- `mirror::taken` (agents) writes a batch a session at a time, each under a savepoint of the batch's one transaction. A session refused by a rule of the mirror's tables (a constraint, a type, a size) or not in the shape of the column lists is rolled back to exactly what the mirror held of it, and named with SQLite's reason. Any other error (the disk, the file) is still the whole batch's.
- The writer commits the rest, tells core of it, logs `mirror refused profile=… session=… why=…` for each one refused, and answers `Wrote::Refused` with their ids.
- The refresh sets a refused session aside with the fingerprint it had, as it does one the far side cannot give: it leaves its queue, the sweep goes on and finishes, and it is asked for again when its fingerprint changes or after a relaunch. Its tokens are not counted until then, and the log says so once for each time it is tried.
- A write that fails for a reason that is not one session's own (a full disk, a mirror that cannot be opened) still ends that tick for the machine's other profiles, as before; the next tick asks again.

**`-B` (finding 4).** The far shell runs `python3 -I -B -`. With `-I` no environment variable can turn Python's cache files off, and a far Python whose own library folder the user may write (a Homebrew one) would write a `.pyc` there for a module whose cache was missing or stale. Apple's Python does not write them either way.

**What a read may write on the far side (findings 1 and 5).** "No intentional write" is the accurate claim, and "writes nothing" is not. From the verifier's lab (`evidence/37/verifier/wal-lab/`: 60 reads a scenario with the reader exactly as the app runs it, under Apple's SQLite 3.54.0 and Homebrew's 3.53.4):

- `state.db` is opened read only and was never changed, in any scenario. A checkpoint, an update and leaving write-ahead mode are all refused on the reader's connection.
- A `state.db-wal` that is there was never changed by a read: not its size, its times or its content.
- `state.db-shm` may be updated or made. A reader of a write-ahead database records its place there, rebuilds the file when it is the first to open the database after a writer that did not close, and makes it where it is missing. That is how SQLite reads such a database; a connection that could not do it (`immutable`) would read a database that is being written wrongly.
- Beside a database that was closed cleanly, with no side files: Apple's SQLite fails to open it (ticket 36's limit), and a non-Apple SQLite makes an empty `-wal` and a `-shm` and leaves them.
- The times that moved on the Mini were Hermes's own gateways writing their logs about once a minute, as said above.

**Tests for three safeguards that had none (finding 3).** The two paces (`plan.rs`, and the lab test above); the sweep's saved place (`a_relaunch_with_a_page_half_written_reads_that_page_again`: a relaunch with one session of a page unwritten reads that page again and ends with all thirty sessions); the first queue's turn-taking (`a_first_queue_longer_than_a_tick_serves_every_session_in_turn`: twelve live sessions, four served a tick, all twelve after three ticks). Each fails with its safeguard broken (`evidence/61/mutations.txt`).

**Not verified here:** the pace on the Mac Mini itself (no contact with it in this ticket: the lab stands in, with 25 idle listed sessions as the Mini has); a real far database that holds two usage rows the mirror cannot tell apart; a far Python that would have written a cache file (the flag is checked, and every cache was already there on this Mac).

### Pace leftovers after 61's verifier (ticket 63)

Ticket 61's verifier passed it with six small findings (`evidence/61/verifier-report.md`). This is what was done with them.

**A session that waits on a request with no card (finding 4).** Hermes 0.22.5 lists a session that waits on a set of questions as `working`, and one that waits on a single question as `waiting` (the recordings `question-batch-timeout.json` and `question-peek-without-subscribing.json`). A set of questions shows a card here, and a card made the session waiting. One that shows none (a request Agent HUD never shows, such as a password, a set it cannot read, or a profile two instances claim) kept the machine's history at the quick pace for as long as it waited. Confirmed in the lab first: a session in a turn with such a request, and nothing else at work, gave two ticks in 3 s at a quick pace of 200 ms.

- Each reading of a session says whether Hermes lists a request open to its viewers (`open_requests`), of a kind shown here or not. The gateway keeps the sessions whose newest reading said so (`asking`); a read that fails leaves that as it was, and a session that is not read (idle) or left the list is not one.
- `Gateway::working` leaves those out. Nothing else reads it: the session's row still says it runs, and the poll interval is as before.
- Once the request is answered, cancelled or timed out, the next reading lists none, and the turn counts again.

**The quick pace ends after a turn (finding 1).** The two minutes a turn keeps the quick pace are `Plan::worked` now (still 120 s; the plan's test holds it), and the lab test `a_turn_that_ended_leaves_the_quick_pace_once_its_bound_has_passed` gives it 3 s: a turn brings the quick pace, and from 8 s after its end no tick comes for 3 s. The live list's own freshness keeps `plan::FRESH`.

**The refused sessions on the status (finding 3).** The refresh counts, by profile, the sessions it set aside because the mirror refused them, and takes one off when it is written. A connected far machine with any says so in its note, in both languages: "1 session's usage is not counted: its records could not be stored." (or "3 sessions' …"). A machine with no history reader keeps that note instead. The count is known since this launch: after a relaunch it is 0 until the sweeps reach each such session again, which the whole sweep does within its 24 hours (about 4 hours on the Mini by ticket 37's receipt).

**The log's reason (finding 5).** A session not in the shape of the column lists is logged `why=not in the shape of the mirror's columns`. A rule of the tables still gives SQLite's own text, which names the table and the column and no value.

**A writer test (finding 2).** `a_session_refused_before_it_had_a_row_gets_its_live_row_once_listed`: refused at its first write, then listed live by a later poll, it has its live row. Taken for a session the mirror holds, it would have none until a relaunch.

**A relay test under load (finding 6).** `a_listener_of_another_user_is_refused_and_no_line_reaches_it` exchanged with the relay under the short 3 s deadline, and a loaded Mac took longer than that to start the relay (`Err(CutOff)`). That one exchange uses the app's 10 s deadline (`RELAY_SECONDS`), as ticket 56 did for `a_ticket_is_in_no_output`; every assertion is as it was. Ticket 59 (item 11) looks at the same file.

**Not verified here:** the pace on the Mac Mini; a real request of Hermes's that shows no card here (the lab's gateway stands in, with a `secret` request); the note in the Settings window (it is the machine's note, which ticket 38's list already shows).

### For tickets 39 and 40

- `hud_rows.held` is set at each tick for the sessions the far `runtime/active_sessions.json` lists with `surface` `desktop`, and cleared for the rest. Core is not told of a change to it alone, and the reader does not read it yet: ticket 39 decides how a held session shows.
- A far session shows its stored tokens at the tick after it was written on the far side: within 60 s while a session is in a turn there and for two minutes after the last one ended, 5 minutes otherwise (a scheduled job that ran and ended between two polls, or a session the gateway never listed in a turn), and about 12 minutes after the first launch against a machine with thousands of sessions.
- The mirrors now hold whole histories. A change to every row at once (a new label) is still read a hundred sessions a pass (ticket 54).
- A profile whose gateway is stopped has no history read where the far Python has Apple's SQLite (ticket 36's limit): its home's reads fail as "state.db could not be opened", its whole check stays as it was, and the other homes are read.

**Tests.** `agents/src/hermes/mirror/history_tests.rs` and `far_history/refresh.rs` (the write and the rules, pure), `core/src/tests/hermes_history.rs` (the ledger), `platform/src/clients/hermes/mirror/tests/history.rs` (the writer with a real core), `history/plan.rs` (the ages), `tests/machines/history.rs`, `history_more.rs`, `history_leftovers.rs` and `history_pace.rs` (the lab: a far database behind the throwaway `sshd`, the machines the app runs, a real core), `tests/far_timing.rs` and `far_rows_timing.rs` (the bounds with reads in flight, by hand), `tests/far_mini_history.rs` (a real far machine, by hand).

**Not verified here:** a far session's stored count moving while its turn runs on a real machine (no session worked on the Mini during the run); a history read with the far gateway stopped; a real database slow enough to need the smaller page (the lab makes one with a far side that waits); a far machine other than the Mini; Linux and Windows; the timing bounds with history reads over a real link, beyond the polls' own time.

## Count calls on a lane of their own, and what changes in a grown mirror (ticket 60)

Ticket 35's verifier found that a slow `session.stats` held up the polls, that a session's last count could be missed, and that a changed row in a grown mirror was listed late. This is what was changed, and what was measured.

### The lane

**Why a lane, and not a limit on each call.** A gateway answers the calls of one socket in turn. A count that is given up after a short wait is still being answered on the polls' socket, and the next poll's list waits behind it. So the calls leave on another socket.

- **The model** (`agents/src/hermes/gateway/sample.rs`). `Step::Stats` is no step of a poll: `Poll::steps` never gives one. `Gateway::count()` begins a round, a `Counting`: every session the newest poll that counted listed as working, and every session owed a count, of the profiles that poll listed. Whoever runs it gives each reply to `answer` or says the call `failed`, takes its `samples`, and hands it back as `Input::Counted`. Only a far gateway that is ready begins one.
- **The lane** (`platform/src/clients/hermes/counting.rs`). A poll that came back with somebody to ask wants one round (`Lane::want`), which starts the lane's thread where it does not run. The lane opens a socket as every socket is opened, asks the round's calls on it, and waits for each reply as long as a call may take (`Pace::answer`, 5 s) from the reply before it. A call never answered is given up, and the socket with it. The samples go to the machine's mirror writer under the route number the lane's socket was opened under (`Writer::counted`); a round read over a link that is gone by then is dropped whole, as a poll is (ticket 58).
- **How often.** One round for each poll at most, one at a time, and after a round the lane rests for as long as that round took. Ten sessions that work still cost ten calls a poll, and a gateway slow to count is asked half of the time at most. A socket that does not open is tried again after 5 s. With no round wanted for 30 s the lane closes its socket and ends; the next poll that wants one starts it.
- **The writer** (`mirror.rs`, `mirror/work.rs`). Counts are a write of their own on the writer's queue. One such write waits for a profile at most, holding the newest of every session any waiting round asked, so a disk that stalls piles nothing up. The 2 s spacing, the route number rules and "a count the mirror holds is not written again" are ticket 35's, unchanged.

**What changed for a row.** A count reaches the writer after the list that gave its session a row, so a new session's row is one commit and its first count the next. Before, the two could be one.

### A session's last count

A session is owed a count when:

- it started, worked or waited at one poll and is listed as something else at the next (waiting is new: a session that waited on a card and whose turn ended before a poll saw it work again was never asked);
- a turn was read to start or end in it by ticket 33's looks (new: a turn that ran wholly between two polls that both list the session as idle).

It stays owed until a call for it is answered, zeros included, and is asked again in the round after each poll: five rounds at most, then it is left until a turn runs in it again. A round that was out while its session came to be owed says nothing of that debt. A session that left the live list is owed nothing, since Hermes holds no agent for it.

### What changes in a grown mirror

| What changed | Passes until it shows, before | Now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One session's count, 1,000 rows | 11 before ticket 54, 1 since | 1 |
| One session's count, 5,000 rows | 51 before ticket 54, 1 since | 1 |
| The machine's label, 1,000 rows | 11, the newest rows last | 1 |
| The machine's label, 5,000 rows | 51, the newest rows last | 1 |

Ticket 54 made a mirror's page look at 10,000 old rows, which already brought a count to one pass, and kept the limit of a hundred sessions read a page. A label changes every row, so it still took a pass for each hundred, two seconds apart in the app: about 100 s at 5,000 rows. A mirror's page now reads as many changed sessions as it looks at. Nothing more is read than before, each session once; it is read in one pass and not fifty. Hermes's own database, whose sessions can hold anything, keeps the hundred.

**Measured** in ticket 34's lab (the app's pace, hints withheld, no file watcher, the collector's turns modelled on the app's), 20 runs each, one case at a time. Least, middle, most, in ms.

| Case | Bound | Rows | Test build (load 7 to 34) | Optimized build, as the app is built (load 10 to 15) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A count: from Hermes saying it to the row showing it | 6 s | 1,000 | 484, 1106, 1836 | 126, 1117, 1855 |
| | | 5,000 | 376, 1119, 1822 | 474, 1189, 1676 |
| A count: from its commit to its listing | 2 s | 1,000 | 70, 80, 156 | 14, 32, 263 |
| | | 5,000 | 303, 336, 503 | 58, 94, 555 |
| A label: from the writer being told to every row showing it | 6 s | 1,000 | 423, 446, 557 | 66, 87, 420 |
| | | 5,000 | 2086, 2151, 3627 | 446, 826, 1403 |
| A label: from its commit to every row showing it | 2 s | 1,000 | 422, 445, 556 | 65, 87, 418 |
| | | 5,000 | **2085, 2151, 3627: all 20 over** | 445, 820, 1402 |

A count's time is mostly the wait for a poll and the writer's 2 s spacing. A label's is the one pass, which reads every row of the mirror: 5,000 rows cost about 2 s of a test build's time and under 1 s of the app's. So the 2 s from commit to listing holds for a label on 5,000 rows in the optimized build and not in a test build. With two cases run at a time at a load of 20 to 100 (the first run, kept), the test build also missed it for a label on 1,000 rows (5 of 20, most 3.5 s) and for a count in 5,000 (3 of 20, most 3.0 s), as ticket 54's own case of a new row did in that run (3 of 20, most 3.4 s); and a label on 5,000 rows took up to 8.6 s. No production timing value was changed.

**Tests.** `agents/src/hermes/gateway/tests/samples.rs` (who is asked, the three ways a last count was missed, a round that was out), `tests/turns.rs`, `transcript_tests.rs` (a mirror's page), `core/src/tests/hermes_live/large.rs` and `hermes_live.rs` (the day totals), `platform/src/clients/hermes/tests/counting.rs` (the lane: held counts, a session read apart, a call never answered, the route number, a lost link, the rest, the end), `mirror/tests/live.rs`, `tests/failed.rs`, `tests/large.rs`, and by hand `tests/far_timing.rs` (`card_and_poll_time_while_counts_are_held`) and `tests/far_rows_timing.rs` (counts and labels at 1,000 and 5,000 rows).

**Not verified here:** a real far machine, where `session.stats` has still not been timed for a session that works (ticket 40); whether a slow count on one socket slows a real gateway's answers on another, which the pretend gateway cannot show; the app's own collector, which the timing cases model from its source; a count that moves while a first sync of thousands of new rows is being read, which waits for that read to end; Linux and Windows.

### Leftovers after 60's verifier (ticket 64)

- **A count before its row.** A round can come back before the list that gives a new session its row reaches the writer (a list write that failed, a list dropped from a full queue while the disk stalls, a poll thread paused between unlocking its model and queuing its list). The count is held, not taken for what the mirror says, and written once a list names its session. After a relaunch, a count that arrives before the first list is held too, even where the mirror has its row from before: that costs one write that changes nothing.
- **The lane and a new route.** The lane looks at the route number while it waits for a round, every 100 ms, and gives its socket up as soon as the number is not the one the socket was opened under. A round already out when the number moves ends as before, and what it read is dropped by the writer.
- **Still for ticket 40:** a working session whose count never answers costs a new socket about every 10 s.

## Desktop-hosted far sessions (ticket 39)

The Hermes desktop app, connected to a far machine over SSH, runs its sessions in a backend of its own there. On Hermes 0.22.5 the gateway does not list such a session and takes no answer for it (ticket 19). Ticket 39's lab found the same on 0.23.1 (below). The rule follows what the gateways show, not the version.

- **Listed decides.** A session in a gateway's live list is an ordinary one, with cards, whatever else is known of it. A session is hosted by the desktop app when ticket 37's refresh last read it in the far `runtime/active_sessions.json` with `surface` `desktop` (Hermes's lease registry, which keys it by its stored id: `tui_gateway/session_lifecycle.py`, `hermes_cli/active_sessions.py`) and no live list a poll gave in the last two minutes lists it. The mirror writer holds both, under one lock (`mirror/desktop.rs`). Each profile is its own. The held list is the refresh's batch as the writer commits it (the same batch that sets `hud_rows.held`), kept in the writer's memory rather than read back from the mirror. So after a relaunch nothing is held until the refresh's first tick reads the file again, 10 s after the machine is followed (`Plan::first`): until then the note is missing. No card depends on it.
- **Such a session** is listed from its stored row, with its machine's label and its state from its stored activity. It never gets a card: only a follower makes cards, and only for what a live list holds. No gateway call names it: the polls name only what a live list holds, and a round of count calls (ticket 60's lane) drops, just before it goes out, any session the newest list no longer holds (`Gateway::still_listed`), since a round is built from one poll and sent a moment later. It is never waiting, since nothing says so.
- **The machines list** notes a connected far machine that has one: "Questions asked there are answered in Hermes desktop." / "在这台机器上提出的问题，请在 Hermes 桌面版中回答。" A missing history reader's note stands alone; otherwise this note comes first, then ticket 63's count of sessions the mirror would not take, joined (`words::far_notes`).
- **Moving.** A held session that a live list then lists gets its card at that poll, and the note goes. One the desktop app lets go of is an ordinary stored session: listed, no note.
- **Where it was started.** Every Hermes row's `origins` comes from its stored `source` where no hook or delivery names it: `desktop` is `desktop:hermes`, `cli` and `tui` are `cli:others`, any other word is `others:<word>`. Before, every Hermes row said `cli:others`. A session this Mac read before this change keeps `cli:others` until it is read again.
- **The way back.** Hermes declares an app link, `hermes://`, verified on macOS only, offered only where the host has it registered. It stands in as the way back of every Hermes session with none better, far or local, and its row carries `navigationLabel`, which the session page shows: "Open in Hermes desktop" / "在 Hermes 桌面版中打开".

**What the link accepts** (Hermes source at `~/.hermes/hermes-agent`, 0.23.1 canary, read only): `apps/desktop/electron/main.ts`, `handleDeepLink` (lines 17388-17476) parses `hermes://<kind>/<name>?<params>`, activates the main window, and hands `{kind, name, params}` to the window (`open-url` on macOS, line 17574). The window (`src/app/contrib/hooks/use-desktop-integrations.ts`, lines 423-505) knows `mcp/install`, `connections/done`, `blueprint`, `plugin`, `plugin-agent`, `plugin-desktop` and `skill` (`src/lib/deeplink-routes.ts`), and otherwise navigates to `hermes://open/<path>` or a plugin's route (`src/lib/hermes-open-target.ts`). A session route is `/<session id>` alone (`src/app/routes.ts`, `sessionRoute`, `routeSessionId`, lines 204-235): no link names a connection, and the app opens a session on whichever connection its own list has it under. So the link cannot open one session on one connection, and the HUD stops at bringing the app forward: `hermes://`, whose empty kind is activated and then ignored.

**Timing.** A desktop-hosted row and its note come at the refresh's tick: within a minute while a far session works, five minutes otherwise (ticket 61's pace), and later during a machine's first whole sweep. The note goes the same way once the desktop app lets go. No bound of the spec covers them. A held session that a gateway then lists gets its card at the next poll, as any listed session does.

**Tests.** `agents/src/naming/surfaces_tests.rs` (`a_hermes_session_was_started_where_its_stored_source_says`), `agents/src/hermes/transcript_tests.rs` (`every_record_says_where_its_session_was_started`), `core/src/tests/hermes_mirror.rs` (`hermes_rows_say_where_they_were_started_and_open_in_the_desktop_app`), `platform/src/navigation/tests.rs` (`hermes_desktop_comes_forward_only_where_its_link_is_registered`), `platform/src/clients/hermes/mirror/tests/desktop.rs` (the writer), `tests/machines/desktop.rs` (the lab: two far machines behind throwaway `sshd`s, the gateways' frame logs), `tests/desktop_words.rs` (both languages, alone in its process), `tests/machines/desktop_real.rs` (by hand: the real-Hermes lab below), `agents/src/hermes/gateway/tests/samples.rs` (`a_round_asks_nothing_of_a_session_a_newer_list_left_out`), `packages/app/test/session-return.test.ts`. The tests' frame log of what the HUD writes to a gateway is `tap::keep_frames` (test builds only).

**The lab with real Hermes.** Ticket 19's recipe on this Mac's Hermes 0.23.1 (gateway `code_version` 0.23.1): one throwaway home with a real gateway and, beside it, `HERMES_DESKTOP=1 hermes serve --isolated --ssh-session-token-file … --ssh-owner-nonce …` as `remote-lifecycle.ts` (`buildSpawnCommand`) starts it, each with a session waiting on a question, reached through the lab's `sshd` as a far machine. The gateway's `session.active_list` never held the backend's session (it lists only its own process's sessions, `tui_gateway/methods_session.py`); the held file had it under its stored id with `surface` `desktop`. The HUD showed the gateway's session with its card and the backend's as a row on `Mini`, `desktop:hermes`, idle, with no card and the note; of the 4,433 frames it wrote to the gateway in that run (3,041 before the resume below, 760 of them naming the gateway's session, and 1,392 after), none named the backend's session. A gateway client's `session.resume` of it was refused (`runtime_coordination_required`), so on 0.23.1 such a session does not move into a gateway's list that way, and nothing changed in the HUD. Two things 0.23.1 needs that 0.22.5 did not, for the lab's own clients only: a client must declare `client.capabilities` with server requests, or a question is dropped unasked; and a gateway prompt needs a `submission_id`.

**A lease the desktop backend leaves.** Hermes prunes an entry of a backend that has died only when it next touches its registry, and the HUD reads the file as it is: such a session stays desktop-hosted, and its machine keeps the note, until then. Telling a dead far process apart would need a change to the history reader, which this ticket does not make.

**Not verified here:** a desktop-hosted session on a real far machine (the lab's backend was started by hand as the desktop app starts one, on loopback, not by the app over SSH); the installed Hermes app's own copy of the link handler, which was not compared with the source beyond `open hermes://` bringing the running app forward. The card's and the completion alert's button kept "Return to session" here; since ticket 65 they read "Open in Hermes desktop" too (row "Hermes app link named on cards and reply alerts").

## App Nap, and the priority the app watches at (ticket 71)

**What was wrong.** macOS runs App Nap on an app none of whose windows can be seen. About half a minute after the last of them is covered, or after the screen locks, every thread of the app goes to priority 4, the lowest band the scheduler has. Nothing is wrong while the Mac is quiet: the polls of a far gateway took 0.1 s for half an hour like that. Once the processors are full of normal work (a build, a test run, several agents), a napped app gets no time at all for tens of seconds. The followers' polls, the hook inbox, the alert tracker and the sounds all run in that one process.

**How it was found out** (macOS 27, an M-series MacBook, screen locked the whole time; every file is in ticket 71's evidence):

| What ran | Priority of every thread, from `ps -M` |
| --- | --- |
| The fork before this change, 30 s after launch | 4 |
| The same build launched with `-NSAppSleepDisabled YES`, macOS's own switch that turns App Nap off for one app | 31 to 54, for the 88 s watched |
| The fork with this change, client hooks on | 20 |
| A bare AppKit app with one window, holding nothing | 4 |
| The same, holding a `background` activity | 4 |
| The same, holding a user-initiated activity, idle sleep allowed or not | 20 |
| The same, holding a user-initiated activity plus `latencyCritical` | 46, as an app in use |

So it is App Nap, and nothing else: the one switch that only turns App Nap off keeps the app where it was. It is not the accessory activation policy: both napped apps were Dock apps at the time, with their Settings windows open behind the lock (`lsappinfo` says `Foreground`). The locked screen matters only because it hides every window.

**What the fork does.** The host tells its shell at every snapshot whether the app watches for requests, which is `connection.clientHooks.enabled`: client hooks are on and their listener runs, and with it every native channel's follower. On macOS the shell then holds one activity with the system (`NSProcessInfo.beginActivity`, `NSActivityUserInitiatedAllowingIdleSystemSleep`, reason "Watching for agents' requests"), begun once however often it is told, and given back when hooks go off. A build that owns no hooks, hooks that failed to go in, hooks turned off and the demo all hold nothing, and the app naps exactly as before.

**What it does not do.** It does not keep the app at the priority of an app in use. macOS still lowers an app nobody can see, to 20 where it was 4. That is enough for what was measured below, and it costs nothing more. Adding `latencyCritical` keeps 46, but asks macOS for exact timers too, which is what App Nap saves battery on; it is left out until a measurement needs it.

**The hand check**, `evidence/71/tools/load-check.sh` (it loads the Mac with `yes > /dev/null` loops, stopped by pid, while a copy of the fork follows a lab Hermes gateway; it reads the fork's own log). Screen locked, 24 loops:

| Check | Bound | Before | With this change |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `busy`: a question waits, so the follower polls every second. 60 polls under load | 19 in 20 within 1 s | 60 polls did not end in 80 s; the three that did took 24.1, 15.4 and 20.8 s | 60 ended in 47 s, mean 61 ms, slowest 546 ms |
| `busy`: 8 more questions asked meanwhile, 7.5 s apart, each left open 6.5 s | a card within 4 s (never over 6 s) | no card for any of the 8 | 21 to 786 ms, middle 626 ms |
| `idle`: nothing waits; one question asked 10 s into 40 s of load | a card within 13 s | 8.7 s, with a poll of 18.1 s in the same run | 664 ms |

Under a real test run too: while `cargo test --workspace` ran on this Mac for five minutes (load 30 rising to 84), the fork with this change, screen locked and at priority 20, made 360 polls of the lab gateway with a question waiting. The slowest took 315 ms. That kind of run is what first stalled the Mac Mini's polls for 23 to 29 s.

**What it costs.** With client hooks on and no gateway to follow, over 240 s with the screen locked: 2.36 s of processor time before and 2.36 s with the change, macOS's energy figure 0.9 for both (`top`), and no power assertion held by the app in either (`IOPMCopyAssertionsByProcess`), so nothing keeps the Mac or its display awake. A unit test holds the real activity and counts the process's power assertions before and while it is held; it fails if the activity is changed to one that keeps the Mac awake.

**Windows and Linux.** Nothing like it was found, and nothing was measured there. By Microsoft's documents Windows does not lower the priority of an app whose windows cannot be seen: on battery it runs it on the efficient cores at their most efficient clock (Low QoS), Windows 11 stops promising it a finer timer than the default, and the low-priority "efficiency mode" is applied only when the user or the app asks. Linux has no App Nap: neither the kernel's scheduler nor systemd's defaults weigh an app by whether its window shows. `shell/windows.rs` and `shell/linux.rs` provide `watching` and do nothing, and say what a Windows adapter would call if polls are ever seen to stall there.

**Not measured.** The screen was locked for the whole of ticket 71, so the priorities with the screen unlocked, with the HUD's panel open or closed on a screen someone looks at, and of the app as an accessory (no Settings window) are not known from this ticket; ticket 40's walk-through reads them. The WebKit helper processes that draw the windows are separate processes with their own throttling; what was measured and changed is the app's own process, where everything that finds a request and plays its sound runs. Upstream's Swift app holds no activity either (no `beginActivity` and no `NSAppSleepDisabled` in its sources); whether macOS naps it was not checked, as the official app is never run.

## A session listed as working for days (ticket 67)

Found on the Mac Mini by ticket 40: its default gateway listed one session as in a turn that had done nothing since 13 November (source `acp`, never ended in the Mini's `state.db`), and for as long as the HUD was open that gateway was polled every 0.8 s with 15 calls in each poll, and the Mini's history was read every 65 s. The HUD showed the session as running.

### What made each pace quick

- **The history's 60 s** came from the session: `Gateway::working` counted it as a turn under way, at every poll.
- **The polls' 0.8 s did not come from the session.** It is the connection's close watch (`Pace::watch`, ticket 13): a gateway that lists any session, idle ones included, is polled at least every 0.8 s, because a turn can begin in any of them with nothing but a poll to tell of it. The Mini's default gateway lists its old chats for as long as it runs. Taking the stuck session out of the model's own wait (`poll_ms`, 1 s or 10 s) changes nothing while that watch holds, so the watch itself had to stop for a gateway where nothing moves.

### The rule

For each listed session the model keeps what the list last said of it, where its stream was last seen (its epoch and latest event, from the reads and looks the polls make anyway), and when either last changed (`gateway/quiet.rs`). A session neither has changed in for the quiet time is **quiet**:

- it does not shorten the model's wait between polls (`poll_ms`);
- it is not at work for a far machine's history (`working`);
- on a far machine it does not keep the close watch (`lively`): that holds only while a card shows or some listed session is not quiet. A gateway of this Mac keeps it while anything is listed, as ticket 13 set it.

It counts again at once when a poll lists it as something else, or finds its stream at another place or in another epoch. A session is seen for the first time when it is first listed, when it is listed again after leaving the list, and after its gateway restarts. **A socket that is cut restarts no clock** (corrected by ticket 72): losing the connection keeps the list and what was seen of it, the first poll after the reconnect goes on from there, and a gateway that was quiet is quiet still. Ticket 67's answer named "every launch or reconnect" as bringing thirty quick minutes; that is true of a launch, of a restart of the gateway and of a follower made anew (a retargeted entry), and not of a reconnect (its verifier: three polls in 25 s after a cut). A read that fails says nothing of where a stream is and is no move, so a session whose stream cannot be read goes quiet by the list alone. Learning where a stream is for the first time is no move either. A session that is read apart from the polls (ticket 30) moves by its own reading.

The rule holds for every listed session, whatever it is listed as: one that only starts (Hermes can list one so for days too), one that waits with no card here, and the idle ones, which is what lets a far gateway's close watch end.

### The quiet time: thirty minutes

`QUIET_MS` is 1,800 s, from Hermes's own source, as the copy in ticket 40's lab home has it (release date 2026.9.24; `hermes_cli/config_defaults.py`, `gateway/run.py`, `run_agent.py`, `agent/tool_executor.py`, `tools/terminal_tool.py`):

- `agent.gateway_timeout` is 1,800 s: a turn in which nothing happens at all for that long is taken for hung and ended by Hermes, which moves its stream.
- One call of a model may take 1,800 s at most (`HERMES_API_TIMEOUT`).
- A terminal command runs in a turn for 600 s at most (`FOREGROUND_MAX_TIMEOUT`); a longer one is moved to the background and the turn goes on.

A tool that runs silent for longer than that (a delegated task has no time limit by default) is kept alive by Hermes's own 30 s heartbeat, which writes nothing on the stream. Its session goes quiet here. That costs it nothing but pace: its cards are found at the slow pace, and its history is read every 5 minutes.

### What does not change

- **The row.** It says what Hermes says: running. The HUD can tell that a stream stands still, not that a turn is over, and a silent tool looks the same. Saying "idle" would be a guess shown as a fact; saying nothing new needs no new state, no words and no change in the UI. The cost that mattered was the pace.
- **Cards.** A quiet session is read at every poll exactly as before. A request asked in it moves its stream, so the poll that finds it also ends the quiet. With no hint from Hermes that poll comes within 10 s: the 13 s bound of spec section 10 for a gateway where nothing is busy. A card that shows always keeps both quick paces.
- **Count calls** (ticket 60's lane): a session listed as working is still asked its count after each poll, now at the slow pace.
- **This Mac's gateways**: polled every 0.8 s while they list anything.

### Measured

In the lab (`tests/machines/quiet.rs`, a quiet time of 4 s, the model's own 1 s and 10 s): a far gateway listing one session as working forever and ten idle ones gives 1 poll and no history tick in 5 s once it is quiet, where the code before gave about 160 polls and 25 ticks; its stream moving brings 20 polls and 5 ticks back within one slow poll; a question asked just after a slow poll came back shows its card in about 10.0 s.

On the Mac Mini, read only (`tests/far_mini_quiet.rs`, the app's own pace, 46 minutes on 19 November, with the session still listed): its default gateway listed 12 sessions, 11 idle and **one as `starting`**, not as working. Hermes lists a turn it took in and whose agent never began so, a poll does not read a session that only starts, and that is why the rule for a request with no card (ticket 63) never saw it. For 30 minutes the model said 1 s, close watch, at work. At 1,808 s it said 10 s, no close watch, not at work, and stayed so.

| On the Mini's default gateway | Before the quiet time | After it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Polls a minute | 74 | 26 |
| Calls a minute (16 in a poll: 4 lists, 12 looks) | 1,184 | 416 |
| The app's `polls n=60` lines | 48 s apart | 2 min 6 s to 2 min 28 s apart |
| History ticks | 60 to 71 s apart | 306 s and 305 s apart |

Ticket 40's HUD, the build before this change, was following the same gateway in the same minutes: its lines stayed 48 s apart. The `reports` gateway, which lists nothing, was polled 13 times a minute throughout.

**What still hurries the Mini's polls.** The 26 polls a minute are not the model's 6. Hermes sends `platforms.changed` on the default gateway's socket about every 20 s, whatever its sessions do, and every hint is followed by a poll at once and five seconds of the close watch (ticket 13's rule, `Pace::warm`): eight polls a hint. Between hints the polls came 10 s apart. The `reports` gateway gets that hint about once a minute, which is all of its 13 against 6. This ticket leaves the hint rule as it is.

**Not verified here:** Linux and Windows; a far machine other than the Mini; a real turn with a tool silent for more than thirty minutes; a session Hermes lists as `working` for days on a real machine (the Mini's is listed as `starting`; the lab's is listed as working).

## One slow call on a quiet far gateway (ticket 72)

Ticket 67 slowed the polls of a far gateway where nothing has moved for thirty minutes to one every 10 s. Spec section 10 gives a card there 13 s, measured from Hermes asking to the HUD showing. With every call answered in time a card shows in 9.9 s. Ticket 67's verifier found what one slow call does to that.

### What was late

A poll goes to the model whole: it is handed over once every call it made is answered or given up, and a call is given up after 5 s (`Pace::answer`). So a card waits for the slowest call of the poll that found its question, whose call that may be.

| The poll that finds the question also has | Card, before | Card, now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| nothing slow | 9.9 s | 9.9 s |
| a read of the session listed as working, slow by 4 s | 13.9 s | 11.9 s |
| the same read, slow by 6 s (longer than a call may take) | 14.9 s by the code; not run | 11.9 s |
| a look at an idle session that is never answered | 14.9 s | 11.9 s |

The card was never lost. Before ticket 67 the same cases took 4.7 s and 0.7 s, because the gateway was polled every 0.8 s; a far gateway that lists nothing always had the 10 s and the same sum.

### The choice

The ticket offered two ways: time the next idle poll from when the last one started and not from when it ended, or cut the wait so the sum stays inside 13 s.

The first changes nothing. The polls are already timed from the start of the one before (`link.rs`, `due = began + wait`, since ticket 13), and the 9.9 s above is that. The lateness is not in the wait. It is the time of the poll that finds the question, and no timing of its start shortens that without polling more often than every 10 s, which is the cost ticket 67 removed.

So the wait is cut, and only where no new card can be waiting on it. Cutting every call's 5 s would also cut the read of the very session that asked, which then fails and is read again later: worse for the one card the bound is about.

### What is asked last, and how long it is waited for

Two kinds of call in a far gateway's poll are for sessions nothing has changed in. (This said "have nothing new to wait for". Ticket 73 corrected it: a set of questions can wait on the first kind. See "A set of questions in a quiet session".)

- the read of a session nothing had changed in for the quiet time when the poll began, and that the list says the same of now (`Gateway::stood`, `Poll::still`);
- the look at an idle session's stream for its turns (ticket 33), on which no card rests.

**They go out last** (`gateway/order.rs`). A poll asks its lists, then the reads of every other session to their end (where the stream is, the search for its oldest event, a snapshot), and only then those. A gateway answers the calls of one socket in turn, so a call that is slow holds back every call sent after it. Asked in the same round, as the looks were, a slow one stood between a card's first read and its second. Asked last, none can.

A quiet session is among the first again as soon as the list says something else of it, which is what a question does (Hermes lists it as waiting), and for as long as a card of it shows.

**They are waited for two seconds** (`platform/src/clients/hermes/waits.rs`, `Pace::still`), all of them together, from the moment the first is asked. After that the poll is handed over with what it has. What did not answer is treated as a call that was never answered always was:

- a look's session rests for 30 s (ticket 33's rule), and its turns are told when it is looked at again (since ticket 73 only the look the gateway was at rests, not the ones that waited behind it);
- a session's read is taken over by a lane (ticket 30's rule), and the polls leave it out (since ticket 73 also a quiet session's read that was left unasked);
- its cards stay as they are, and the next poll asks again.

A snapshot of a session that is asked last (one that has waited for thirty minutes with no card) gets what is left of the two seconds, and is not taken once they are over; the next poll takes it.

Two seconds because the sum is the bound: 10 s between two polls, 2 s, and the 1 s section 10 gives a poll for its own work. `pace.rs` holds that arithmetic in a test. Two seconds is also eight times what already counts as slow for a read (`Pace::apart`, 250 ms).

### A session read apart, at the pace of the polls

The verifier's second finding. A session whose read is slow gets a lane (ticket 30), and a lane read its session again as soon as the last read ended, or every 0.8 s, for as long as the polls wanted it read. Being quiet changed nothing for it: on a gateway polled three times in 30 s, the session listed as working for days was read ten times, 3 s each, one read after another.

A lane now reads no more often than its gateway is polled (`apart.rs`, `every`): the close watch (0.8 s) while the model keeps it, and the model's own wait otherwise, which is 10 s on a far gateway where nothing moves. It looks every 250 ms whether that has changed, so the moment a poll or its own read finds something moving it is back at the quick pace.

A lane ended when no poll had wanted its session for six close-watch polls and one call (9.8 s). Quiet polls come 10 s apart, so a lane could end between two of them, and the next poll would read the slow session itself. That count is now six polls at the pace they come at.

### What does not change

- **The paces.** 1 s and 10 s in the model, 0.8 s for the close watch, and a poll due from the start of the one before.
- **A quiet session is read at every poll**, as ticket 67 left it. A read that the two seconds cut short is read by its lane.
- **Every call a new card may wait on has its 5 s**: the lists, and the reads and snapshots of every session that is not asked last.
- **This Mac's gateways.** Their polls ask nothing last (`Gateway::stood` is empty for them), and they look at no idle session.
- **A poll with nothing slow in it** makes the same calls. A far gateway's looks are one round later than they were where the poll also reads a session for its cards.

### What can still be late

- **The session that asks is itself slow to read, or its profile's list is.** Its card cannot show before its own list and reads are answered. That is so at every pace and on every gateway; on a quiet far gateway it starts from 10 s.
- **A question in a quiet session that is read apart** (listed as working for days and slow to read) shows within the slow pace and one read of its own: the lane reads it every 10 s now, where it read it back to back. With reads of 3 s that is up to 13.3 s, against about 6 s before. This is the cost of the second finding's fix, and it applies only while nothing else moves on that gateway.
- **Two slow things in one poll**, one among the first calls and one among the last, add up.

### Measured

`tests/machines/quiet_slow.rs`, the verifier's probe made tests: the app's own waits (`Pace::default()`), a quiet time of 4 s for the app's thirty minutes, the lab of `machines/quiet.rs` with ten idle sessions and one listed as working, and three idle sessions more whose ids sort after it, so that the asking session's read is tried on both sides of the slow call. Each question is asked 150 ms after a poll began, with no hint.

| Case | Before this ticket | Now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A read turns slow by 4 s, asker sorts before the slow session | 13,887 ms | 11,872 ms |
| The same, asker sorts after | (the test stops at the first) | 11,884 ms |
| A read turns slow by 6 s | (not reached) | 11,866 ms |
| A look is never answered, three sessions in turn | 14,873 ms | 11,880, 11,889 and 11,878 ms |
| The poll that found the question took | 4,003 and 5,003 ms | 2,001 to 2,003 ms |
| Reads apart of a quiet session slow by 3 s, in 30 s | 10 (and 3 polls) | 3 (and 3 polls) |
| A card beside that session | (not reached) | 9,860 ms, no poll held |
| Reads apart in the 8 s after something moved on that gateway | (not reached) | 2: the lane is back at the quick pace within 250 ms |

**Not verified here:** a real far machine, and with it whether a real gateway answers the calls of one socket in turn (the design assumes it does, as tickets 30 and 60 did, and the order costs nothing if it does not); Linux and Windows.

## A set of questions in a quiet session (ticket 73)

Found by ticket 72's verifier. Ticket 72 put the reads of quiet sessions and the looks at idle ones last in a far gateway's poll, in one round, with two seconds for all of it, on the ground that no new card can be waiting on either.

### The card that did wait

Hermes lists a session as waiting while a question or an approval waits in it, and that moves its read among the first. A set of questions (`clarify_batch`) is different: Hermes goes on listing the session as working (ticket 11), and the set is found only in the answer to the session's read (`open_requests`). It may open with no event on the stream. So for a session listed as working for days, nothing tells the poll that anything changed, and its read stays among the last.

A session's read is two calls at the least: where the stream is, then its events. In ticket 72's order the first of them went out in one round with the looks. One look that was never answered held that round until the two seconds were up, the second call was never asked, and the card waited for the next poll, ten seconds later.

| A set of questions in a quiet session, with no event | Before ticket 72 | With ticket 72 | Now |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| nothing slow | 9.9 s | 9.9 s | 9.9 s |
| a look at an idle session is never answered | 14.9 s | 19.9 s | 11.9 s |
| the same, with an event beside the set | 14.9 s | 11.9 s | 11.9 s |
| another quiet session's read is slow by 4 s | 13.9 s | 12.0 s | 12.0 s |
| another quiet session's read is never answered | not measured | 19.9 s | 12.0 s |

The first two columns of the first four rows are the verifier's (`evidence/72/verifier-report.md`). The last row is a case this ticket found. The card was never lost.

### The order now

A far gateway's poll asks (`gateway/order.rs`):

1. its lists;
2. the reads of every session that is not quiet, each to its end, with 5 s for each round;
3. the reads of the quiet sessions, each to its end;
4. the looks at idle sessions, and the reads that follow a look.

Steps 3 and 4 share the two seconds (`Pace::still`), counted from the first call of step 3. A look that is slow, or never answered, now has no read waiting on its round. In the lab the quiet sessions' reads take a few milliseconds, the unanswered look takes the rest of the two seconds, and the poll is handed over with the set: 11.9 s.

### What the two seconds leave behind

When the time is up, three kinds of call are left (`platform/src/clients/hermes/waits.rs`, `polls.rs`).

- **The call the gateway was at.** A gateway answers the calls of one socket in turn, so of the calls never answered the first one asked is the one that was slow (`unanswered`). A look: its session rests 30 s. A read: its session is read apart.
- **Calls that only waited behind it.** They were sent in the same round and are unanswered too.
  - A look does not rest any more. Before, every look left unanswered rested 30 s, so a turn that ended in a session whose look only waited was told a rest later: 12.0 s late in the lab, where a rest is 12 s, and 30 s or more in the app. It is told at the next poll now (7 ms after the slow call let go, in the lab).
  - A read is still read apart, after the slow one. The verifier listed this as a cost (its finding 3: a session whose read only queued gets a lane). It is kept on purpose. The poll's socket is busy with the slow call, so only a socket of its own can read that session in time: the lane showed the card in 12.0 s. Left to the next poll it took 19.9 s in the lab (break m13). A lane costs a thread and a socket for three reads, then the polls take the session back. What changed is the order: the slow session gets its lane first, where the order was left to chance, so it is not the one left out when more than four are cut at once.
- **Reads that were never sent.** A quiet session's next read is asked only once its round is over, so a round that runs into the two seconds leaves it unasked. It is read apart too. Without this, a set of questions waited for the next poll whenever another quiet session's first call was the slow one: 19.9 s. `Poll::give_up` gives up what a poll had left to ask, in the order it would have asked, and returns it.

### A lane no poll reads the session of

Ticket 72 kept a lane for six polls at the pace they come at, so that a lane on a quiet far gateway lives from one poll to the next, ten seconds on. That also applied to a lane whose session had left the list or gone idle: no poll will want it read again, and it was kept for 65 s where it ended after 9.8 s before. It sent nothing in that time, but held a thread, a socket and one of the four lanes, on this Mac too once its list emptied. Such a lane ends after six close-watch polls again (`apart.rs`, `kept`). A lane whose session a poll would still read (listed as working or waiting, or showing a card) is kept as ticket 72 left it.

### The verifier's four smaller findings

| Finding | What was done |
| --- | --- |
| A look cut at 2 s tells its turn's end 30 s or more later | Fixed for the looks that only waited behind the slow call, which was every look sent after it. They no longer rest. Lab test `a_look_that_only_waited_behind_a_slow_one_does_not_rest`. The one look that was itself slow still rests, as ticket 33 set it: that is what keeps it from holding up every poll after |
| Treating every far session as quiet is not caught by the model tests | Model test `a_session_that_is_not_quiet_is_read_among_the_first_though_the_list_says_the_same_of_it`. The verifier's break fails it |
| A quiet session whose read only queued behind a slow call also gets a lane | Kept, with the reason above and a break that shows the 19.9 s without it. The slow one now gets its lane first (unit test `the_calls_never_answered_are_told_in_the_order_they_were_asked`) |
| A lane of a session that left the list idles for 65 s, on this Mac too | Fixed: 9.8 s again. Unit test `a_lane_no_poll_would_read_the_session_of_ends_at_the_close_watch` |

### What does not change

- The paces, the two seconds and the five. `Pace` has no new value.
- Ticket 72's three lab tests and ticket 67's tests, which pass as they were written.
- A poll with nothing slow in it makes the same calls. Where it reads a quiet session and looks at idle ones, the looks are one or two rounds later.
- This Mac's gateways: their polls ask nothing last and look at nothing.

### What can still be late

- **More quiet sessions cut short at once than there are lanes.** A gateway has four. A fifth quiet session whose read was cut short waits for the next poll, where the slow one is out of the way.
- **Many quiet sessions that are each a little slow.** Their reads share the two seconds. If they need more together, the ones left over go to lanes at every poll, four at a time.
- **A look that had little of the two seconds left**, because the quiet sessions' reads used most of them, rests as a slow one does if it was first in its round.
- Everything ticket 72 lists under "What can still be late".

### Measured

`tests/machines/quiet_set.rs`, the verifier's probe made tests, in the lab of `quiet_slow.rs` with one more session listed as working (`a-stuck`, which sorts before `s-stuck`). The waits are the app's own; the quiet time is 4 s. Each set opens 150 ms after a poll began, with no hint.

| Case | With ticket 72 | Now |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nothing slow | 9,852 ms | 9,874 ms |
| A look never answered, set in `s-stuck`, stream where it was | 19,897 ms | 11,852 ms, nobody read apart |
| The same, set in `a-stuck` | (the test stops at the first) | 11,881 ms |
| A look never answered, an event beside the set | 11,890 ms (the verifier) | 11,857 ms |
| The other quiet session's read slow by 4 s, set in `s-stuck` | 12,028 ms, 2 read apart | 12,033 ms, 2 read apart |
| The same, an event beside the set | 12,039 ms | 11,996 ms |
| The same, set in `a-stuck` | 11,869 ms, 1 read apart | 11,863 ms, 1 read apart |
| The other quiet session's read never answered, set in `a-stuck` | 19,882 ms | 12,024 ms, 2 read apart |
| The same, set in `s-stuck` | (not reached) | 12,041 ms, 2 read apart |
| A turn that ended behind a held look is told (`tests/far/looks.rs`, a rest of 12 s) | 12,015 ms after | 7 ms after |

**Not verified here:** a real far machine, and whether a real gateway answers the calls of one socket in turn (if it answers them side by side, each poll finds one slow call of several, and the others at the polls after); a real set of questions asked with no event (the one recording has a `tool.start` before it); Linux and Windows.
## Languages (ticket 66)

Upstream 0.6.7 writes its text in nine languages (`d31510c1`). Text is written in English in the code (`tr!("…")` in Rust, `t("…")` in TypeScript) and looked up in the catalog of the current language, `src/packages/protocol/locales/<code>.json`: English, Simplified and Traditional Chinese, Japanese, Korean, French, German, Spanish and Brazilian Portuguese. `l10n::text(zh, en)` is still in upstream's crate as a transitional helper, and upstream's own code no longer calls it.

**What the fork does.** Every string the fork adds is a key in those catalogs, like upstream's own. The fork's rule "text in both languages" is now "text in the catalogs": a fork string is written once in English, with `tr!` or `t`, and has an entry in each of the eight catalogs that are not English. No fork code calls `l10n::text`, and no Chinese is left in the fork's source outside tests.

**English has no entry for most keys, as upstream has it.** The key is the English text, and a key that a catalog lacks shows as itself, so `en.json` holds only what differs from its key: the forms of a counted key, and the words of a key that carries a note in brackets. Of the fork's keys that is three: `Needs you [sound]`, `Finished [sound]`, and the counted one with its `_one` and `_other`. The other fork keys are not in `en.json`, and `check-l10n` does not ask for them there (it skips English).

**The seven other languages: translated, by machine.** A key missing from a catalog does show in English at run time (`l10n::tr_in` falls back to the key, and i18next to `en`). But upstream's check, `node src/tooling/check-l10n.mjs`, refuses any catalog that lacks a key the code uses, and refuses Chinese left in source. So an English fallback is not open to the fork without failing upstream's check or editing it. The coordinator's ruling for that case (2026-11-22): fill the other catalogs with careful translations of the fork's short strings and say here that they are machine-translated.

- **Simplified Chinese** (`zh-Hans.json`) holds the fork's own Chinese, word for word as it was in the code before this sync.
- **Traditional Chinese, Japanese, Korean, French, German, Spanish and Brazilian Portuguese** were translated by the agent that did this sync (Claude), following the wording upstream's catalogs already use (session, gateway, sign-in, the formal address each catalog uses, French spacing before a colon). **No speaker of those languages has read them.** They are short interface strings. Correct one by editing its line in the catalog; nothing else reads it.

Two keys carry a note in brackets, as upstream's own do (`"Back [step]"`), because the bare word could mean something else in another place: `Needs you [sound]` and `Finished [sound]`, the two rows of the Sound section. English shows them without the note (`en.json`).

One string is counted: "1 session's usage is not counted…" and "3 sessions' usage is not counted…" are one key with `_one` and `_other` forms (and `_many` in French, Spanish and Portuguese), chosen by `tr!(count = …)`.

A string held as data cannot be a bare `&str` any more, because the check wants every key written as a literal inside `tr`. The Hermes app link's name (`AppNavigation.label`, ticket 65) is therefore a function that returns the words, not a pair of strings.

**The check.** `check-l10n` is not part of `pnpm run check` upstream, and no workflow runs it. Run it by hand after any change to text (it is step 4 of "Updating from upstream" now). It passed at this sync: 1,552 keys, no key missing from any of the eight catalogs it checks, no key in a catalog that no code uses, no Chinese in source. It finds text only where it is written through `tr` or `t`: an English literal shown as it is passes it unseen.

**Text that is still a literal in English.** The host's notes on a login item or on sound that Settings does not show as they are: `launchAtLogin.notice` on Windows and Linux and where there is no app bundle (upstream's own, and Settings shows its own line in their place), and `sound.notice` on Windows and Linux (the fork's; Settings says "Sound isn't played on this system yet." from the catalogs in its place, `sound-reason.ts`). They were left as literals because this Mac builds only the macOS target, so a change to `host/src/system/windows.rs` or `linux.rs` could not be compiled here. A failed save's reason from the host ("macOS could not update the login item…") is a literal too, as upstream's errors are.

**The fork's keys, all machine-translated in the seven languages above** (64 keys):

- `Sound`
- `Play sounds`
- `Needs you [sound]`
- `Finished [sound]`
- `Machines`
- `Last contact {{age}}`

`check:file-size` fails on upstream's `host/src/hud/macos.rs`, fixed after 0.6.7. ## Updating from upstream

Remotes: `origin` is this fork, `upstream` is the author's repo with pushing disabled.

1. Fetch both remotes: `git fetch upstream && git fetch origin`.
2. Fast-forward the fork's `main` to upstream: `git push origin upstream/main:main`. This must be a fast-forward. If it is refused, someone committed to `main`; move that commit to `fork` first.
3. Bring `fork` up to date by merging, not rebasing: `git checkout fork && git merge --ff-only origin/fork && git merge origin/main`, fix any conflicts, then `git push origin fork`. Merging keeps `fork` history stable, so open ticket branches built on it stay valid and no force push to a shared branch is needed.
4. Run the checks:

   ```sh
   pnpm install --frozen-lockfile
   pnpm run format
   pnpm run check
   pnpm run test
   pnpm run lint
   cargo test --workspace
   node src/tooling/check-l10n.mjs
   git diff --check
   ```

   `check-l10n` is upstream's check of the catalogs (section "Languages"). It is not part of `pnpm run check`, so run it by itself.

5. Rebuild and reinstall: `sh src/tooling/fork-install.sh`. It builds the bundle with the fork's overlay and `AGENT_HUD_HOOK_OWNER` set to the installed copy's executable (so its client hooks switch works), quits the running fork, replaces `/Applications/Agent HUD Fork.app` and opens it (`--no-open` skips that). It always builds in this checkout's own `target/`: a build folder shared between worktrees can reuse another worktree's crates.
